在友情那流露于外的亲昵与温柔中，她不偏不倚地向他们三个人“送秋波”。但有时，当她正给塔菲或领主做模特时，小比利从画布前抬起头，会发现她那双灰色的眼睛正凝视着自己，目光包容一切，如此透彻、深沉、无法言喻地甜美、和善与温柔，带着一种深情款款、如鸽子般温存柔和的关切，以至于小比利会感到一阵心头悸动，手抖得握不住画笔；他在恍惚中会忆起，在他还是个小男孩、母亲还是个未经愁苦与悲伤侵袭的年轻美人时，母亲也常常这样看着他；而那滴总在小比利眼角随时准备落下的泪珠，便极难克制在原处——忍住不流下来。
每当此时，一想到特里尔比竟然做全裸模特，那念头就宛如一把利刃刺透他的心。
诚然，她并不随意给任何相邀之人做模特。但她依然给迪里安做模特；给伟大的热罗姆（Gérôme）做模特；给几乎不用其他模特的卡雷尔先生（M. Carrel）做模特。
可怜的特里尔比拥有一种令人伤感的出类拔萃，她凌驾于所有模特之上，正如卡吕普索（Calypso）超拔于她的众仙女；无论是在石膏模像还是在丰满鲜活的肉身中，她那高贵的头颅、优美的双肩，以及修长匀称的四肢，早已闻名于巴黎的每一间画室。
因此，每当斯文加利带着玩世不恭的冷笑走进来为他们弹琴时，小比利总是难以忍受；若斯文加利逮着小比利落单，便会以极其令人作呕的口吻对特里尔比的身材评头论足，逼得小比利不得不走出房间去避开他。

===Original===

But she was destined soon to know both fear and shame.
And here it would not be amiss for me to state a fact well known to all painters and sculptors who have used the nude model (except a few senile pretenders, whose purity, not being of the right sort, has gone rank from too much watching), namely, that nothing is so chaste as nudity. Venus herself, as she drops her garments and steps on to the model-throne, leaves behind her on the floor every weapon in her armory by which she can pierce to the grosser passions of man. The more perfect her unveiled beauty, the more keenly it appeals to his higher instincts. And where her beauty fails (as it almost always does somewhere in the Venuses who sit for hire), the failure is so lamentably conspicuous in the studio light﻿—the fierce light that beats on this particular throne﻿—that Don Juan himself, who has not got to paint, were fain to hide his eyes in sorrow and disenchantment, and fly to other climes.
All beauty is sexless in the eyes of the artist at his work﻿—the beauty of man, the beauty of woman, the heavenly beauty of the child, which is the sweetest and best of all.
Indeed it is woman, lovely woman, whose beauty falls the shortest, for sheer lack of proper physical training.
As for Trilby, G﻿—﻿—, to whom she sat for his *Phryne*, once told me that the sight of her thus was a thing to melt Sir Galahad, and sober Silenus, and chasten Jove himself﻿—a thing to Quixotize a modern French masher! I can well believe him. For myself, I only speak of Trilby as I have seen her﻿—clothed and in her right mind. She never sat to me for any Phryne, never bared herself to me, nor did I ever dream of asking her. I would as soon have asked the Queen of Spain to let me paint her legs! But I have worked from many female models in many countries, some of them the best of their kind. I have also, like Svengali, seen Taffy “trying to get himself clean,” either at home or in the swimming-baths of the Seine; and never a sitting woman among them all who could match for grace or finish or splendor of outward form that mighty Yorkshireman sitting in his tub, or sunning himself, like Ilyssus, at the Bains Henri Quatre, or taking his running header *à la hussarde*, off the springboard at the Bains Deligny, with a group of wondering Frenchmen gathered round.

===Chinese===

然而她注定很快便要体味到恐惧与羞耻。
在此，我也许不妨陈述一个所有雇用过人体模特的画家与雕塑家都深知的事实（除开少数几个道貌岸然的老伪君子，他们的纯洁并非出于正道，早已因过度的窥视而变得腐败变质）：那就是世间没有任何事物能像赤裸那般圣洁。维纳斯（Venus）自身在褪去衣衫登上模特台时，便已将武器库中用以挑动凡人粗鄙欲望的每一种武器都弃置在地。她展露的美越是完美无瑕，就越是强烈地激发人类崇高的本能。而在她的形体有所欠缺之处（那些受雇出任维纳斯的女子身上几乎总有欠缺），在画室的光线——即猛烈照射在这座特殊宝座上的强光之下，这种缺憾显得如此扎眼而刺目，哪怕唐璜（Don Juan）亲临（既然他无需作画），也会在悲哀与幻灭中遮住双眼，逃往别处。
在作画的艺术家眼中，一切美都是超越性别的——男性的美、女性的美，以及孩童那宛如天赐、最甜美崇高的美。
事实上，正是女人，这可爱的女人，纯粹由于缺乏得当的体魄锻炼，在形体美上反而最为逊色。
至于特里尔比，请她为自己的《芙里尼》（*Phryne*）出任模特的画家G——曾告诉我，她当年的模样足以融化加拉哈德爵士（Sir Galahad），令西勒诺斯（Silenus）酒醒，教朱庇特（Jove）本人肃然起敬——甚至能让现代法国的风流浪子化身唐·吉诃德！我完全相信他的话。至于我自己，我只谈论我所见到的特里尔比——衣冠齐整、神智清醒时的她。她从未为任何芙里尼给我做过模特，从未在我面前袒露身体，我也从未妄想提出这种要求。我还不如去恳求西班牙王后让我描绘她的双腿呢！但我在许多国家与众多女模特合作过，其中有些堪称同类中的佼佼者。我也曾像斯文加利那样，见过塔菲在寓所或在塞纳河的露天浴场里“洗净身子”；而在所有女模特中，没有一个人能在体态的优雅、匀称或雄伟上，比得上那个坐在大澡盆里的健硕的约克郡汉子，比得上他在亨利四世浴场（Bains Henri Quatre）像伊利索斯（Ilyssus）般沐浴阳光，或是如骠骑兵般（*à la hussarde*）自德里尼浴场（Bains Deligny）的跳板上助跑俯冲扎入水中，引得周围一群法国人啧啧称奇。

===Original===

Up he shot himself into midair with a sounding double downward kick, parabolically; then, turning a splendid semi-demi-summersault against the sky, down he came headlong, his body straight and stiff as an arrow, and made his clean hole in the water without splash or sound, to reappear a hundred yards farther on!
“*Sac à papier! quel gaillard que cet Anglais, hein?*”
“*A-t-on jamais vu un torse pareil!*”
“*Et les bras, donc!*”
“*Et les jambes, nom d’un tonnerre!*”
“*Mâtin! J’aimerais mieux être en colère contre lui qu’il ne soit en colère contre moi!*” etc., etc., etc.
---
*Omne ignotum pro magnifico!*
If our climate were such that we could go about without any clothes on, we probably should; in which case, although we should still murder and lie and steal and bear false witness against our neighbor, and break the Sabbath day and take the Lord’s name in vain, much deplorable wickedness of another kind would cease to exist for sheer lack of mystery; and Christianity would be relieved of its hardest task in this sinful world, and Venus Aphrodite (alias Aselgeia) would have to go a-begging along with the tailors and dressmakers and boot-makers, and perhaps our bodies and limbs would be as those of the Theseus and Venus of Milo; who was no Venus, except in good looks!
At all events, there would be no cunning, cruel deceptions, no artful taking in of artless inexperience, no unduly hurried waking-up from Love’s young dream, no handing down to posterity of hidden uglinesses and weaknesses, and worse!
And also many a flower, now born to blush unseen, would be reclaimed from its desert, and suffered to hold its own, and flaunt away with the best in the inner garden of roses!
And here let me humbly apologize to the casual reader for the length and possible irrelevancy of this digression, and for its subject. To those who may find matter for sincere disapprobation or even grave offence in a thing that has always seemed to me so simple, so commonplace, as to be hardly worth talking or writing about, I can only plead a sincerity equal to theirs, and as deep a love and reverence for the gracious, goodly shape that God is said to have made after His own image for inscrutable purposes of His own.
Nor, indeed, am I pleading for such a subversive and revolutionary measure as the wholesale abolition of clothes, being the chilliest of mortals, and quite unlike Mr.

===Chinese===

他双腿在空中利落地连蹬两下，呈抛物线腾空跃入半空；接着以天空为幕翻了一个绝妙的四分之一前滚翻，笔直如箭般俯冲直下，入水处干脆利落，未激起半点水花与声响，直到100码（100 yards）开外才重新露出水面！
“活见鬼！这英国佬真是条硬汉，哈？（*Sac à papier! quel gaillard que cet Anglais, hein?*）”
“谁见过这么一副躯干啊！（*A-t-on jamais vu un torse pareil!*）”
“还有那双胳膊！（*Et les bras, donc!*）”
“还有那双腿，真是神了！（*Et les jambes, nom d’un tonnerre!*）”
“天哪！我宁可跟他发火，也绝不愿惹他冲我发火！（*Mâtin! J’aimerais mieux être en colère contre lui qu’il ne soit en colère contre moi!*）”诸如此类，不一而足。
---
凡未知者皆奇伟！（*Omne ignotum pro magnifico!*）
倘若我们的气候允许人们不着寸缕地四处走动，我们大概真会这么做；若真如此，尽管我们依然会杀戮、撒谎、偷盗、作假见证陷害邻舍、破坏安息日以及妄称主的名，但另一种令人痛心的罪恶却会因为神秘感的彻底消失而不复存在；基督教在这个罪恶尘世中最艰巨的任务亦将得以卸下，阿佛洛狄忒·维纳斯（又名阿塞尔吉亚）恐怕不得不与裁缝、女装裁缝和鞋匠一同沿街乞讨，而我们的身躯与四肢或许就能像忒修斯（Theseus）与米洛的维纳斯那般健美——那位米洛的雕像除了相貌出众之外，其实根本算不得真正的维纳斯！
无论如何，世间将不再有狡黠残酷的欺瞒，不再有对单纯懵懂者的巧取豪夺，不再有从爱情清梦中过于仓促的惊醒，也不再有隐蔽丑陋与虚弱传给后代的恶果，甚至更糟的行径！
而且，许多如今生在荒野、孤芳自赏的花朵，也将从荒漠中被救赎出来，得以自立于世，在最内层的玫瑰园里与百花争妍斗艳！
在此，请允许我向偶然翻阅本书的读者致以谦卑的歉意，因为这段题外话既长，又可能离题万里，且主题如此特殊。对于那些在我向来认为极其朴素、平凡、几乎不值一提或落笔成文的事情中发现由衷反感甚至深感冒犯的读者，我只能表明自己拥有与他们同等的诚意，以及对上帝据说依自身形象、为不可揣度的目的而创造的优雅美好体态持有同样深沉的爱与敬畏。
当然，我绝非在此鼓吹彻底废除衣裳这样颠覆性与革命性的举措，因为我是最怕冷的凡夫俗子，丝毫不像忒修斯先生或是

===Original===

Theseus or Mr. Ilyssus either.
---
Sometimes Trilby would bring her little brother to the studio in the Place St. Anatole des Arts, in his *beaux habits de Pâques*, his hair well curled and pomatumed, his hands and face well washed.
He was a very engaging little mortal. The Laird would fill his pockets full of Scotch goodies, and paint him as a little Spaniard in *Le Fils du Toreador*, a sweet little Spaniard with blue eyes, and curly locks as light as tow, and a complexion of milk and roses, in singular and piquant contrast to his swarthy progenitor.
Taffy would use him as an Indian club or a dumbbell, to the child’s infinite delight, and swing him on the trapeze, and teach him *la boxe*.
And the sweetness and fun of his shrill, happy, infantile laughter (which was like an echo of Trilby’s, only an octave higher) so moved and touched and tickled one that Taffy had to look quite fierce, so he might hide the strange delight of tenderness that somehow filled his manly bosom at the mere sound of it (lest Little Billee and the Laird should think him goody-goody); and the fiercer Taffy looked, the less this small mite was afraid of him.
Little Billee made a beautiful watercolor sketch of him, just as he was, and gave it to Trilby, who gave it to le père Martin, who gave it to his wife with strict injunctions not to sell it as an old master. Alas! it *is* an old master now, and Heaven only knows who has got it!
Those were happy days for Trilby’s little brother, happy days for Trilby, who was immensely fond of him, and very proud. And the happiest day of all was when Trois Angliches took Trilby and Jeannot (for so the mite was called) to spend the Sunday in the woods at Meudon, and breakfast and dine at the *garde champêtre’s*. Swings, peepshows, donkey-rides; shooting at a mark with crossbows and little pellets of clay, and smashing little plaster figures and winning macaroons; losing one’s self in the beautiful forest; catching newts and tadpoles and young frogs; making music on mirlitons. Trilby singing “Ben Bolt” into a mirliton was a thing to be remembered, whether one would or no!
Trilby on this occasion came out in a new character, *en demoiselle*, with a little black bonnet, and a gray jacket of her own making.

===Chinese===

伊利索斯先生那般抗冻。
---
有时特里尔比会把她的小弟弟带到圣阿纳托尔艺术广场的画室来，孩子穿上了他的复活节盛装（*beaux habits de Pâques*），头发卷得好好的，涂了发膏，小手和脸颊也洗得干干净净。
他是个惹人怜爱的小家伙。领主会把他的口袋塞满苏格兰糖果，并把他画成《斗牛士之子》（*Le Fils du Toreador*）里的小西班牙男孩——一个有着蓝眼睛、浅如麻线般卷发、白里透红面庞的可爱小西班牙人，与他那皮肤黝黑的父亲形成了奇特而别具风味的对照。
塔菲则把他当作印度木棒或哑铃举来荡去，逗得孩子乐不可支；塔菲还会抱他在吊架上荡秋千，教他拳击（*la boxe*）。
孩子那尖脆、欢快、充满稚气的笑声（听起来宛如特里尔比笑声的回响，只是高了整整一个八度）是如此甜美逗趣，教人动容、感动又开怀，以至于塔菲不得不摆出一副凶神恶煞的神情，好掩饰仅仅听到这笑声便不知为何充溢在他男子气胸膛里的那股奇特温柔（唯恐小比利和领主会觉得他婆婆妈妈）；然而塔菲的神情越凶，这小不点儿反而越不怕他。
小比利为他画了一幅极美的写生水彩肖像，如实勾勒出他的模样，并将画送给了特里尔比；特里尔比转赠给了马丁老爹（le père Martin），马丁老爹又交给了自己的妻子，并严词嘱咐她绝不能当作古代名家之作卖掉。唉！如今那画可真成了名家之作了，只有老天知道它落到了谁的手里！
那些日子是特里尔比小弟弟的快乐时光，也是特里尔比的快乐日子，她对他极尽疼爱，又倍感骄傲。而最快乐的一天，莫过于三位英国佬带着特里尔比和让诺（小家伙的名字）去默东（Meudon）的森林里度周日，并在乡村治安员（*garde champêtre*）的屋子里用早点和晚餐。荡秋千、西洋镜、骑毛驴；用十字弓和泥弹打靶，击碎小石膏人偶赢取杏仁饼；迷失在美丽的森林深处；捉蝾螈、蝌蚪和小青蛙；吹响纸哨（*mirlitons*）奏出乐声。特里尔比对着纸哨歌唱《本·博尔特》（“Ben Bolt”），那情景无论谁想不想记起，都叫人永生难忘！
在这次郊游中，特里尔比以一幅崭新的姿态亮相——俨然一位大家闺秀（*en demoiselle*），戴着一顶黑色小女帽，身穿一件自己亲手缝制的灰色短上衣。

===Original===

To look at (but for her loose, square-toed, heelless silk boots laced up the inner side), she might have been the daughter of an English dean﻿—until she undertook to teach the Laird some favorite cancan steps. And then the Laird himself, it must be admitted, no longer looked like the son of a worthy, God-fearing, Sabbath-keeping Scotch solicitor.
This was after dinner, in the garden, at *la loge du garde champêtre*. Taffy and Jeannot and Little Billee made the necessary music on their mirlitons, and the dancing soon became general, with plenty also to look on, for the *garde* had many customers who dined there on summer Sundays.
It is no exaggeration to say that Trilby was far and away the belle of that particular ball, and there have been worse balls in much finer company, and far plainer women!
Trilby lightly dancing the cancan (there are cancans and cancans) was a singularly gainly and seductive person﻿—*et vera incessu patuit dea*! Here, again, she was funny without being vulgar. And for mere grace (even in the cancan), she was the forerunner of Miss Kate Vaughan; and, for sheer fun, the precursor of Miss Nelly Farren!
And the Laird, trying to dance after her (“*dongsong le konkong*,” as he called it), was too funny for words; and if genuine popular success is a true test of humor, no greater humorist ever danced a pas seul.
What Englishmen could do in France during the fifties, and yet manage to preserve their self-respect, and even the respect of their respectable French friends!
“*Voilà l’espayce de hom ker jer swee!*” said the Laird, every time he bowed in acknowledgment of the applause that greeted his performance of various solo steps of his own﻿—Scotch reels and sword-dances that come in admirably.﻿ ﻿…
Then, one fine day, the Laird fell ill, and the doctor had to be sent for, and he ordered a nurse. But Trilby would hear of no nurses, not even a Sister of Charity! She did all the nursing herself, and never slept a wink for three successive days and nights.
On the third day the Laird was out of all danger, the delirium was past, and the doctor found poor Trilby fast asleep by the bedside.
Madame Vinard, at the bedroom door, put her finger to her lips, and whispered: “*Quel bonheur! il est sauvé, M. le Docteur; écoutez! il dit ses prières en Anglais, ce brave garçon!

===Chinese===

乍看之下（除开她那双侧面系带、宽松方头、没有鞋跟的丝绸靴子），她俨然一位英国教长千金的模样——直到她开始教领主跳几段她最得意的康康舞步。而到了那时，必须承认，领主自己看起来也再不像一位品行端正、敬畏上帝、谨守安息日的苏格兰事务律师之子了。
这是晚饭后在乡村治安员小屋（*la loge du garde champêtre*）的花园里发生的事。塔菲、让诺和小比利吹起纸哨为他们伴奏，舞蹈很快演变成全场共舞，周围也聚拢了许多看客，因为在夏日的周日，治安员这里总有大批食客用餐。
毫不夸张地说，特里尔比绝对是那场舞会上艳压群芳的焦点；在更体面的交际圈里，多得是气氛更逊色、女子相貌平平得多的舞会！
特里尔比轻盈跳起康康舞（康康舞与康康舞之间自是大有不同）的姿态极其优雅诱人——步履之间，俨然真女神降临（*et vera incessu patuit dea*）！在此，她同样滑稽可笑却绝不粗俗下流。若单论舞姿之曼妙优雅（即便是在康康舞中），她堪称凯特·沃恩小姐（Miss Kate Vaughan）的先驱；而若单论逗趣欢腾，她更是内莉·法伦小姐（Miss Nelly Farren）的鼻祖！
至于领主学着她的样子跟跳（他管这叫“跳个康康舞”[*dongsong le konkong*]），那副滑稽模样简直难以言喻；若说博得大众由衷的喝彩是检验幽默的真准绳，那么古往今来绝没有任何大幽默家跳出的独舞（*pas seul*）能出其右。
想想50年代的英国人在法国能干出些什么名堂，却仍能保全自己的尊严，甚至赢得受人尊敬的法国朋友的钦佩！
“瞧瞧我这是个什么样的人！（*Voilà l’espayce de hom ker jer swee!*）”每逢领主自创的一套套独舞步法——穿插得恰到好处的苏格兰里尔舞和剑舞——博得满堂喝彩而鞠躬致意时，他总会冒出这么一句。……
随后，在一个晴朗的日子里，领主病倒了，不得不请来医生，医生吩咐要雇个看护妇。可特里尔比坚决不同意请看护，甚至连仁爱修女会的修女也不行！她独自承担了全部照料护理，连续3天3夜未曾合眼。
到了第3天，领主脱离了一切危险，谵妄高烧已退，医生在床头发现可怜的特里尔比正沉沉熟睡。
维纳尔太太在卧室门口把手指竖在唇边，低声耳语道：“多幸运啊！他有救了，医生先生；听啊！这好小伙在用英语做祷告呢！

===Original===

*”
The good old doctor, who didn’t understand a word of English, listened, and heard the Laird’s voice, weak and low, but quite clear, and full of heartfelt fervor, intoning, solemnly:

> “Green herbs, red peppers, mussels, saffron,

Soles, onions, garlic, roach, and dace﻿—

All these you eat at Terré’s Tavern

In that one dish of bouillabaisse!”

===Chinese===

”
好心的老医生一句英语也听不懂，他侧耳倾听，只听见领主的声音虚弱低沉，却十分清晰，满含发自肺腑的虔诚，正庄严地低吟着：

> “青绿香草、红辣椒、贻贝、藏红花，

鳎目鱼、洋葱、大蒜、斜齿鳊与雅罗鱼——

在戴莱酒馆里你能享用到的这一切，

全都盛在这一道马赛鱼汤里！”

===Original===

“*Ah! mais c’est très bien de sa part, ce brave jeune homme! rendre grâces au ciel comme cela, quand le danger est passé! très bien, très bien!*”
Sceptic and Voltairian as he was, and not the friend of prayer, the good doctor was touched, for he was old, and therefore kind and tolerant, and made allowances.
And afterwards he said such sweet things to Trilby about it all, and about her admirable care of his patient, that she positively wept with delight﻿—like sweet Alice with hair so brown, whenever Ben Bolt gave her a smile.
All this sounds very goody-goody, but it’s true.
So it will be easily understood how the trois Angliches came in time to feel for Trilby quite a peculiar regard, and looked forward with sorrowful forebodings to the day when this singular and pleasant little quartet would have to be broken up, each of them to spread his wings and fly away on his own account, and poor Trilby to be left behind all by herself. They would even frame little plans whereby she might better herself in life, and avoid the many snares and pitfalls that would beset her lonely path in the quartier latin when they were gone.
Trilby never thought of such things as these; she took short views of life, and troubled herself about no morrows.
There was, however, one jarring figure in her little fool’s paradise, a baleful and most ominous figure that constantly crossed her path, and came between her and the sun, and threw its shadow over her, and that was Svengali.
He also was a frequent visitor at the studio in the Place St. Anatole, where much was forgiven him for the sake of his music, especially when he came with Gecko and they made music together. But it soon became apparent that they did not come there to play to the three Angliches: it was to see Trilby, whom they both had taken it into their heads to adore, each in a different fashion:
Gecko, with a humble, doglike worship that expressed itself in mute, pathetic deference and looks of lowly self-depreciation, of apology for his own unworthy existence, as though the only requital he would ever dare to dream of were a word of decent politeness, a glance of tolerance or goodwill﻿—a mere bone to a dog.
Svengali was a bolder wooer.

===Chinese===

“啊！这好小伙真了不起！（*Ah! mais c’est très bien de sa part, ce brave jeune homme!*）大难刚过就这般向苍天感恩！（*rendre grâces au ciel comme cela, quand le danger est passé!*）太好了，太好了！（*très bien, très bien!*）”
好心的老医生虽是个怀疑论者和伏尔泰信徒，素来不热衷祈祷，此刻却深受感动；因为他年事已高，因而宽厚包容，体谅人心。
事后他对特里尔比说了许多赞美之词，赞扬她对病人的悉心照料，教她欣喜得直掉眼泪——宛如当年每当本·博尔特展露笑颜，棕发可人的爱丽丝那般。
这一切听来未免有些过于温情脉脉，却是千真万确。
因此不难理解，随着时光流逝，三位英国佬对特里尔比萌生出了一种非同寻常的情感；他们带着悲伤的预感，料想到这奇妙而愉悦的四人组合终有一天要分道扬镳，每个人都得展翅高飞各奔前程，而可怜的特里尔比将被孤零零地抛在身后。他们甚至为她拟定了种种小计划，好让她能在生活中改善境遇，并在他们离去后避开拉丁区孤独旅途中可能遭遇的重重陷阱与险滩。
特里尔比却从未想过这些长远打算；她看待人生鼠目寸光，从不为明日的忧患烦扰。
然而，在她那个虚幻快活的小天地里，出现了一个不和谐的身影，一个险恶而凶兆毕露的身影不断挡在她前行的路上，遮蔽了她的阳光，将阴影笼罩在她身上——那便是斯文加利。
他也是圣阿纳托尔广场画室的常客，画室里的人看在他的音乐才华份上，对他许多行径都加以宽容，尤其是当他与盖科结伴前来合奏时更是如此。但很快大家便看出，他们来画室并非为了演奏给三位英国佬听，而是为了见特里尔比；他们俩都不约而同地爱上了她，只是各以截然不同的方式：
盖科带着一种卑微如犬的崇拜，流露在沉默而凄凉的恭顺中，带着自惭形秽的眼神，仿佛在为自己不值一提的卑微存在致歉，似乎他敢于梦想的唯一回报，不过是一句体面的客套话，一瞥宽容或善意的目光——如同施舍给狗的一根骨头。
斯文加利则是个更大胆放肆的追求者。

===Original===

When he cringed, it was with a mock humility full of sardonic threats; when he was playful, it was with a terrible playfulness, like that of a cat with a mouse﻿—a weird ungainly cat, and most unclean; a sticky, haunting, long, lean, uncanny, black spider-cat, if there is such an animal outside a bad dream.
It was a great grievance to him that she had suffered from no more pains in her eyes. She had; but preferred to endure them rather than seek relief from him.
So he would playfully try to mesmerize her with his glance, and sidle up nearer and nearer to her, making passes and counterpasses, with stern command in his eyes, till she would shake and shiver and almost sicken with fear, and all but feel the spell come over her, as in a nightmare, and rouse herself with a great effort and escape.
If Taffy were there he would interfere with a friendly “Now then, old fellow, none of that!” and a jolly slap on the back, which would make Svengali cough for an hour, and paralyze his mesmeric powers for a week.
Svengali had a stroke of good-fortune. He played at three grand concerts with Gecko, and had a well-deserved success. He even gave a concert of his own, which made a furor, and blossomed out into beautiful and costly clothes of quite original color and shape and pattern, so that people would turn round and stare at him in the street﻿—a thing he loved. He felt his fortune was secure, and ran into debt with tailors, hatters, shoemakers, jewellers, but paid none of his old debts to his friends. His pockets were always full of printed slips﻿—things that had been written about him in the papers﻿—and he would read them aloud to everybody he knew, especially to Trilby, as she sat darning socks on the model-throne while the fencing and boxing were in train. And he would lay his fame and his fortune at her feet, on condition that she should share her life with him.
“*Ach, himmel*, Drilpy!” he would say, “you don’t know what it is to be a great pianist like me﻿—*hein*! What is your Little Billee, with his stinking oil-bladders, sitting mum in his corner, his mahlstick and his palette in one hand, and his twiddling little footle pig’s-hair brush in the other! What noise does *he* make?

===Chinese===

当他奴颜婢膝时，那是一种饱含冷嘲威胁的虚伪谦卑；当他嬉闹调笑时，那是一种猫捉老鼠般可怕的嬉弄——一只古怪笨拙且污秽不堪的猫；一只黏糊糊、阴魂不散、瘦长古怪的黑色蜘蛛猫，如果恶梦之外真有这种怪物的话。
她再也没有患眼痛，这成了他极大的憾事。其实她疼过，但宁可硬撑着忍受，也绝不向他求助解脱。
于是他常常半开玩笑地试图用目光对她施催眠术，悄无声息地越靠越近，双手做出催眠的手势来回挥动，眼中透出严厉的威慑命令，直到她浑身战栗发抖，几乎吓得犯恶心，几乎感到魔咒像恶梦般笼罩全身，才拼尽全力挣脱清醒过来落荒而逃。
要是塔菲在场，他就会友善地横插一杠：“嘿，老兄，少来这一套！”随后在他背上狠狠拍上一巴掌，震得斯文加利咳嗽上一小时，催眠功力瘫痪上一星期。
斯文加利交了鸿运。他与盖科合办了3场大型音乐会，获得了当之无愧的成功。他甚至还开了一场个人专场音乐会，引起了轰动，并换上了色彩、款式与花纹都独树一帜的华丽昂贵衣裳，引得街上的路人纷纷回头侧目注视他——这正是他最爱的排场。他觉得自己已经飞黄腾达，便在裁缝、帽商、鞋匠和珠宝商那里赊欠了一屁股债，却唯独不肯偿还欠朋友们的老账。他的口袋里总是塞满了剪报纸条——报纸上撰写的关于他的赞誉之词——他逢人便大声朗读给熟人听，尤其是念给坐在模特台上补袜子、身旁正进行着击剑和拳击训练的特里尔比听。他许诺只要她肯与他共度此生，他便把自己的名声与财富悉数奉献在她的脚下。
“天哪，特里尔比（Drilpy）！”他会说，“你根本不知道像我这样一位伟大的钢琴家意味着什么——哈！你那个小比利算个什么东西，整天守着发臭的颜料皮囊，不吭一声地蜷缩在角落里，一手拿着画杖和调色板，另一手捏着那根毫无用处的细小猪鬃画笔瞎鼓捣！*他*又能掀起多大的动静？

===Original===

When his little fool of a picture is finished he will send it to London, and they will hang it on a wall with a lot of others, all in a line, like recruits called out for inspection, and the yawning public will walk by in procession and inspect, and say ‘damn!’ Svengali will go to London *himself*. Ha! ha! He will be all alone on a platform, and play as nobody else can play; and hundreds of beautiful *Engländerinnen* will see and hear and go mad with love for him﻿—Prinzessen, Comtessen, Serene English Altessen. They will soon lose their Serenity and their Highness when they hear Svengali! They will invite him to their palaces, and pay him a thousand francs to play for them; and after, he will loll in the best armchair, and they will sit all round him on footstools, and bring him tea and gin and *küchen* and marrons glacés, and lean over him and fan him﻿—for he is tired after playing them for a thousand francs of Chopin! Ha, ha! I know all about it﻿—*hein*?
“And he will not look at them, even! He will look inward, at his own dream﻿—and his dream will be about Drilpy﻿—to lay his talent, his glory, his thousand francs at her beautiful white feet!
“Their stupid, big, fat, towheaded, putty-nosed husbands will be mad with jealousy, and long to box him, but they will be afraid. *Ach!* those beautiful *Anglaises*! they will think it an honor to mend his shirts, to sew buttons on his pantaloons; to darn his socks, as you are doing now for that sacred imbecile of a Scotchman who is always trying to paint toreadors, or that sweating, pigheaded bullock of an Englander who is always trying to get himself dirty and then to get himself clean again!﻿—*e da capo!*
“*Himmel!* what big socks are those! what potato-sacks!
“Look at your Taffy! what is he good for but to bang great musicians on the back with his big bear’s paw! He finds that droll, the bullock!﻿ ﻿…
“Look at your Frenchmen there﻿—your damned conceited *verfluchte* pig-dogs of Frenchmen﻿—Durien, Barizel, Bouchardy! What can a Frenchman talk of, *hein*? Only himself, and run down everybody else! His vanity makes me sick! He always thinks the world is talking about *him*, the fool! He forgets that there’s a fellow called *Svengali* for the world to talk about! I tell you, Drilpy, it is about *me* the world is talking﻿—me and nobody else﻿—me, me, me!
“Listen what they say in the *Figaro*” (reads it).

===Chinese===

等他那幅傻里傻气的小画画完，他把它寄到伦敦，他们就会把它挂在墙上，同许多别的画挤作一排，宛如列队受阅的新兵，打着哈欠的公众鱼贯走过，打量几眼，骂一声‘该死！’而斯文加利将*亲自*去伦敦。哈哈！他将独自一人屹立在舞台上，弹奏出无人能及的琴音；成百上千美丽的英国女人（*Engländerinnen*）会看着他、听着他，为他神魂颠倒——公主、女伯爵、显赫的英国殿下！一旦听了斯文加利的琴声，她们的从容庄严和崇高头衔立刻就会荡然无存！她们会邀请他去宫殿，奉上1000法郎（1000 francs）请他为她们弹奏；弹完后，他将慵懒地仰卧在最好的安乐椅上，她们团团围坐在脚凳上，为他奉上红茶、金酒、甜糕点（*küchen*）和糖渍栗子（marrons glacés），俯下身为他扇风——因为他刚刚为了1000法郎给她们弹了一整场肖邦，累坏了！哈哈！我对这一切了如指掌——哈？
“而且他甚至都懒得多看她们一眼！他将收回目光望向内心的梦想——而他的梦想里全都是特里尔比（Drilpy）——要把自己的才华、自己的荣光、自己的1000法郎统统奉献在她美丽的雪白玉足之下！
“她们那些愚笨、肥胖、顶着一头黄毛、长着烂泥鼻子的丈夫们会嫉妒得发狂，巴不得狠狠揍他一顿，可他们却不敢。哎！那些美丽的英国女人（*Anglaises*）！她们会把替他补衬衫、在他裤子上缝扣子视作无上的光荣；会视替他补袜子为荣幸，就像你现在为那个整天琢磨着画斗牛士的苏格兰蠢才、或是那个浑身淌汗、顽固如牛、整天把自己弄得一身脏又洗得一身干净的英国佬补袜子一样！——周而复始（*e da capo*）！
“老天！那是多大的袜子啊！简直像装土豆的麻袋！
“瞧瞧你的塔菲！除了用他那大熊爪子拍伟大音乐家的后背，他还会干些什么！这头蠢牛还自以为挺逗！……
“再瞧瞧你那些法国佬——你那些该死自负、该下地狱（*verfluchte*）的法国猪狗——迪里安、巴里泽尔、布夏尔迪！一个法国佬能聊出什么花样来，哈？无非是吹嘘他自己，贬低所有人！他的虚荣心真叫我犯恶心！那个傻瓜总以为全世界都在谈论*他*！他忘了个叫*斯文加利*的人才是全世界该谈论的焦点！我告诉你，特里尔比，全世界谈论的正是*我*——除了我没有别人——是我，是我，是我！
“听听《费加罗报》（*Figaro*）是怎么说的”（念报纸）。

===Original===

“What do you think of that, *hein*? What would your Durien say if people wrote of *him* like that?
“But you are not listening, *sapperment*! great big she-fool that you are﻿—sheep’s-head! *Dummkopf! Donnerwetter!* you are looking at the chimney-pots when Svengali is talking! Look a little lower down between the houses, on the other side of the river! There is a little ugly gray building there, and inside are eight slanting slabs of brass, all of a row, like beds in a school dormitory, and one fine day you shall lie asleep on one of those slabs﻿—you, Drilpy, who would not listen to Svengali, and therefore lost him!﻿ ﻿… And over the middle of you will be a little leather apron, and over your head a little brass tap, and all day long and all night the cold water shall trickle, trickle, trickle all the way down your beautiful white body to your beautiful white feet till they turn green, and your poor, damp, draggled, muddy rags will hang above you from the ceiling for your friends to know you by; drip, drip, drip! But you will have no friends.﻿ ﻿…
“And people of all sorts, strangers, will stare at you through the big plate-glass windows﻿—Englanders, chiffonniers, painters and sculptors, workmen, pioupious, old hags of washerwomen﻿—and say, ‘Ah! what a beautiful woman was that! Look at her! She ought to be rolling in her carriage and pair!’ And just then who should come by, rolling in his carriage and pair, smothered in furs, and smoking a big cigar of the Havana, but Svengali, who will jump out, and push the canaille aside, and say, ‘Ha! ha! that is la grande Drilpy, who would not listen to Svengali, but looked at the chimney-pots when he told her of his manly love, and﻿—’ ”
“Hi! damn it, Svengali, what the devil are you talking to Trilby about? You’re making her sick; can’t you see? Leave off, and go to the piano, man, or I’ll come and slap you on the back again!”
Thus would that sweating, pigheaded bullock of an Englander stop Svengali’s lovemaking and release Trilby from bad quarters of an hour.
Then Svengali, who had a wholesome dread of the pigheaded bullock, would go to the piano and make impossible discords, and say: “Dear Drilpy, come and sing ‘Pen Polt’! I am thirsting for those so beautiful chest notes! Come!”

===Chinese===

“你觉得怎么样，哈？如果人们也这么报道你的迪里安，他会怎么说？
“可你根本没在听，见鬼（*sapperment*）！你这大号的笨雌鹿——羊脑袋！蠢货（*Dummkopf*）！该死（*Donnerwetter*）！斯文加利说话的时候，你居然在看对面的烟囱管！往房屋中间稍微低一点的地方看，在河对岸！那里有一栋丑陋的灰色小楼，里面排着8块（8）倾斜的黄铜板，整整齐齐排成一列，就像学校宿舍里的床铺，终有那么一天，你会沉睡在其中一块铜板上——你，特里尔比（Drilpy），因为不肯听从斯文加利，所以永远失去了他！……你的腰腹中间会搭着一块小皮革围兜，头顶上悬着一只小黄铜水龙头，冰冷的水整日整夜滴滴答答、滴滴答答地流淌，流遍你美丽的雪白身躯，一直流到你美丽的雪白玉足，直到它们泡得发青发绿，而你那些可怜、潮湿、破烂、沾满泥浆的破布衣裳会悬挂在天花板下，供你的朋友辨认你；水珠滴答、滴答、滴答！可你根本不会有任何朋友。……
“形形色色的陌生人会透过大块平板玻璃窗盯着你看——英国佬、拾荒者、画家和雕塑家、工人、步兵小兵蛋子、洗衣老太婆——他们会说：‘啊！那是多么美丽的女人啊！瞧瞧她！她本该乘着双马马车招摇过市的！’而就在这时，谁会裹着厚厚的皮草、抽着哈瓦那大雪茄，乘着双马马车招摇而过呢？除了斯文加利还会有谁！他会跳下马车，把那些下等人（canaille）推到一旁，说道：‘哈哈！那就是大名鼎鼎的特里尔比（la grande Drilpy），她不肯听从斯文加利，反而在他向她倾吐男人的炽热爱意时去看烟囱管，结果——’”
“嗨！见鬼去吧，斯文加利，你到底在跟特里尔比胡扯些什么浑话？你把她恶心成什么样了，你看不见吗？赶紧给我打住，去弹你的钢琴，老兄，否则我又要过去照着你后背来上一巴掌了！”
那个满身大汗、顽固如牛的英国佬就是这样打断斯文加利的求爱，将特里尔比从难挨的煎熬时刻中解救出来的。
于是，对这头顽固壮牛心怀由衷敬畏的斯文加利便会悻悻走到钢琴前，弹奏出令人无法忍受的不协和音，嚷道：“亲爱的特里尔比，来唱一曲《本·博尔特》（Pen Polt）吧！我正渴望听到那美妙绝伦的胸腔共鸣音呢！来吧！”

===Original===

Poor Trilby needed little pressing when she was asked to sing, and would go through her lamentable performance, to the great discomfort of Little Billee. It lost nothing of its grotesqueness from Svengali’s accompaniment, which was a triumph of cacophony, and he would encourage her﻿—“*Très pien, très pien, ça y est!*”
When it was over, Svengali would test her ear, as he called it, and strike the C in the middle and then the F just above, and ask which was the highest; and she would declare they were both exactly the same. It was only when he struck a note in the bass and another in the treble that she could perceive any difference, and said that the first sounded like père Martin blowing up his wife, and the second like her little godson trying to make the peace between them.
She was quite tone-deaf, and didn’t know it; and he would pay her extravagant compliments on her musical talent, till Taffy would say: “Look here, Svengali, let’s hear *you* sing a song!”
And he would tickle him so masterfully under the ribs that the creature howled and became quite hysterical.
Then Svengali would vent his love of teasing on Little Billee, and pin his arms behind his back and swing him round, saying: “*Himmel!* what’s this for an arm? It’s like a girl’s!”
“It’s strong enough to paint!” said Little Billee.
“And what’s this for a leg? It’s like a mahlstick!”
“It’s strong enough to kick, if you don’t leave off!”
And Little Billee, the young and tender, would let out his little heel and kick the German’s shins; and just as the German was going to retaliate, big Taffy would pin *his* arms and make him sing another song, more discordant than Trilby’s﻿—for he didn’t dream of kicking Taffy; of that you may be sure!
Such was Svengali﻿—only to be endured for the sake of his music﻿—always ready to vex, frighten, bully, or torment anybody or anything smaller and weaker than himself﻿—from a woman or a child to a mouse or a fly.

===Chinese===

可怜的特里尔比一被叫唱歌根本无需多劝，便开始她那令人难堪的演唱，让小比利听得坐立难安。在斯文加利的伴奏下，她的歌声显得更加怪诞荒唐，那伴奏简直是不协和音的巅峰杰作，而斯文加利还会给她打气鼓劲——“好极了，好极了，就是这样！（*Très pien, très pien, ça y est!*）”
唱完后，斯文加利便会按他所谓的规矩“测试她的听力”，先弹一下中央C，再弹紧挨在上面的F，问她哪一个音更高；而她则一口咬定两个音完全一模一样。只有当他弹一个低音区音符和一个高音区音符时，她才能听出差别，并说前一个声音听起来像马丁老爹在训斥老婆，后一个声音听起来像她的小教子在劝架调解。
她是个十足的五音不全者，自己却浑然不知；而斯文加利却对她的音乐天赋极尽阿谀奉承之能事，直到塔菲看不下去说道：“听着，斯文加利，让我们听*你*唱一首吧！”
说着，塔菲便精妙绝伦地挠他的肋下，逗得这个家伙嗷嗷怪叫，变得歇斯底里。
随后斯文加利又把捉弄人的兴致发泄在小比利身上，把他的双手反剪在背后旋转着他嚷道：“天哪（*Himmel*）！这算什么胳膊？软得像个女孩子！”
“画画足够使了！”小比利回敬道。
“那这算什么腿？细得像根画杖！”
“你要是再不撒手，踢你足够用了！”
年少娇弱的小比利便猛地踢出脚后跟，狠狠踢向这个德国佬的小腿胫骨；正当德国佬准备报复还击时，大块头塔菲便反剪住*他的*双臂，逼着他再唱一首歌，比特里尔比唱的还要刺耳难听——因为他压根儿没胆量去踢塔菲，这一点大家尽可放心！
斯文加利就是这么个人——仅仅因为他的音乐才华才被大家容忍——他时刻准备着去招惹、恐吓、欺凌或折磨任何比他弱小的人物或生灵——上至女人孩童，下至老鼠苍蝇。

## Part III / 第三部分
===Original===

> “Par deçà, ne dela la mer

Ne sçay dame ni damoiselle

Qui soit en tous biens parfaits telle﻿—

C’est un songe que d’y penser:

Dieu! qu’il fait bon la regarder!”

===Chinese===

> “无论在海这头，还是海那端，

我从未见过哪位贵妇或淑女

能在美德上如此完满——

念及此处便宛如置身梦幻：

天哪！看着她是多么赏心悦目！”

===Original===

One lovely Monday morning in late September, at about eleven or so, Taffy and the Laird sat in the studio﻿—each opposite his picture, smoking, nursing his knee, and saying nothing. The heaviness of Monday weighed on their spirits more than usual, for the three friends had returned late on the previous night from a week spent at Barbizon and in the forest of Fontainebleau﻿—a heavenly week among the painters: Rousseau, Millet, Corot, Daubigny, let us suppose, and others less known to fame this day. Little Billee, especially, had been fascinated by all this artistic life in blouses and sabots and immense straw hats and panamas, and had sworn to himself and to his friends that he would some day live and die there﻿—painting, the forest as it is, and peopling it with beautiful people out of his own fancy﻿—leading a healthy outdoor life of simple wants and lofty aspirations.
At length Taffy said: “Bother work this morning! I feel much more like a stroll in the Luxembourg Gardens and lunch at the Café de l’Odéon, where the omelets are good and the wine isn’t blue.”
“The very thing I was thinking of myself,” said the Laird.
So Taffy slipped on his old shooting-jacket and his old Harrow cricket cap, with the peak turned the wrong way, and the Laird put on an old greatcoat of Taffy’s that reached to his heels, and a battered straw hat they had found in the studio when they took it; and both sallied forth into the mellow sunshine on the way to Carrel’s. For they meant to seduce Little Billee from his work, that he might share in their laziness, greediness, and general demoralization.
And whom should they meet coming down the narrow turreted old Rue Vieille des Mauvais Ladres but Little Billee himself, with an air of general demoralization so tragic that they were quite alarmed. He had his paintbox and field-easel in one hand and his little valise in the other. He was pale, his hat on the back of his head, his hair staring all at sixes and sevens, like a sick Scotch terrier’s.
“Good Lord! what’s the matter?” said Taffy.
“Oh! oh! oh! she’s sitting at Carrel’s!”
“Who’s sitting at Carrel’s?”
“Trilby! sitting to all those ruffians! There she was, just as I opened the door; I saw her, I tell you! The sight of her was like a blow between the eyes, and I bolted! I shall never go back to that beastly hole again!

===Chinese===

九月下旬一个晴朗的周一上午，大约11点左右，塔菲和领主正坐在画室里——各自面对着自己的画作，抽着烟，抱着膝盖，一言不发。周一的沉闷比平时更沉重地压在他们心头，因为前一天深夜，三位好友才刚结束在巴比松（Barbizon）和枫丹白露森林（Fontainebleau）度过的一周回到画室——那是在一众画家中间度过的如天堂般的一周：不妨设想，有卢梭、米勒、柯罗、多比尼，以及其他如今没那么名声显赫的画家。尤其是小比利，深深着迷于这种穿着粗布工装、踏着木屐、戴着硕大草帽与巴拿马草帽的艺术生活，他曾向自己和朋友们发誓，总有一天要在这片土地上了此残生——如实描绘这座森林，并在其中点缀他幻想出的美丽人物——过一种需求简单、志向高尚、有益身心的户外生活。
终于，塔菲开口道：“今天上午去他的干活吧！我现在更想去卢森堡公园（Luxembourg Gardens）散散步，然后在奥德翁咖啡馆（Café de l’Odéon）吃午饭，那儿的煎蛋卷地道，葡萄酒也不是兑水发青的劣酒。”
“我想的正巧也是这桩事，”领主说。
于是塔菲套上他那件旧猎装夹克，戴上他的旧哈罗公学板球帽，帽檐故意歪着戴；领主则穿上塔菲一件长及脚跟的旧大衣，戴上一顶他们接手画室时在屋里找到的破烂草帽；两人一同迈步走进温煦的阳光里，朝卡雷尔（Carrel）的画室走去。因为他们打算把小比利从画架旁诱拐出来，好让他也加入到他们的偷懒、贪吃与彻底放纵之中。
然而，当他们走下那条带有小角楼的狭窄古老街道——莫韦-拉德尔老街（Rue Vieille des Mauvais Ladres）时，迎面遇上的不是别人，正是小比利本人，他浑身散发着一种悲壮的颓唐之气，令他们大吃一惊。他一手拎着颜料箱和写生画架，另一手提着小旅行皮箱。他面色苍白，帽子推在脑后，头发凌乱蓬乱得像一只生病的苏格兰梗犬。
“老天爷！出什么事了？”塔菲问。
“噢！噢！噢！她在卡雷尔那里做模特呢！”
“谁在卡雷尔那里做模特？”
“特里尔比！正在给那帮无赖做全裸模特！我一推开门，她就在那儿；我亲眼看见她了，我告诉你！看见她的那一瞬间，就像当头挨了一记闷棍，我拔腿就逃！我再也不会回那个鬼地方去了！

===Original===

I’m off to Barbizon, to paint the forest; I was coming round to tell you. Goodbye!﻿ ﻿…”
“Stop a minute﻿—are you mad?” said Taffy, collaring him.
“Let me go, Taffy﻿—let me go, damn it! I’ll come back in a week﻿—but I’m going now! Let me go; do you hear?”
“But look here﻿—I’ll go with you.”
“No; I want to be alone﻿—quite alone. Let me go, I tell you!”
“I shan’t let you go unless you swear to me, on your honor, that you’ll write directly, you get there, and every day till you come back. Swear!”
“All right; I swear﻿—honor bright! Now there! Goodbye﻿—goodbye; back on Sunday﻿—goodbye!” And he was off.
“Now, what the devil does all that mean?” asked Taffy, much perturbed.
“I suppose he’s shocked at seeing Trilby in that guise, or disguise, or unguise, sitting at Carrel’s﻿—he’s such an odd little chap. And I must say, I’m surprised at Trilby. It’s a bad thing for her when we’re away. What could have induced her? She never sat in a studio of that kind before. I thought she only sat to Durien and old Carrel.”
They walked for a while in silence.
“Do you know, I’ve got a horrid idea that the little fool’s in love with her!”
“I’ve long had a horrid idea that *she’s* in love with *him*.”
“That would be a very stupid business,” said Taffy.
They walked on, brooding over those two horrid ideas, and the more they brooded, considered, and remembered, the more convinced they became that both were right.
“Here’s a pretty kettle of fish!” said the Laird﻿—“and talking of fish, let’s go and lunch.”
And so demoralized were they that Taffy ate three omelets without thinking, and the Laird drank two half-bottles of wine, and Taffy three, and they walked about the whole of that afternoon for fear Trilby should come to the studio﻿—and were very unhappy.
---
This is how Trilby came to sit at Carrel’s studio:
Carrel had suddenly taken it into his head that he would spend a week there, and paint a figure among his pupils, that they might see and paint with﻿—and if possible like﻿—him. And he had asked Trilby as a great favor to be the model, and Trilby was so devoted to the great Carrel that she readily consented. So that Monday morning found her there, and Carrel posed her as Ingres’s famous figure in his picture called *La Source*, holding a stone pitcher on her shoulder.
And the work began in religious silence.

===Chinese===

我要回巴比松去画森林；我正要过来跟你们说一声。再见！……”
“等一下——你疯了吗？”塔菲抓住他的衣领说。
“放开我，塔菲——该死，放开我！我一周后（1 week）会回来的——但我现在就得走！放手；听见没有？”
“可你听我说——我陪你一起去。”
“不；我想一个人呆着——彻底一个人。放手，我跟你说！”
“除非你凭名誉向我发誓，到了那儿立刻写信，而且在回来之前每天都要写，否则我绝不放你走。发誓！”
“好；我发誓——以名誉起誓！行了吧！再见——再见；周日回来——再见！”说完他便一溜烟跑了。
“这到底是怎么一回事？”塔菲大惑不解地问。
“我猜他是看到特里尔比那副模样，或者说伪装、抑或毫无着装地坐在卡雷尔那里，受刺激了——他这小家伙脾气古怪得很。而且说实话，我也对特里尔比感到意外。咱们一离开对她可真没好处。究竟是什么促使她去的？她以前从没去过那种集体画室做模特。我一直以为她只给迪里安和老卡雷尔做私人模特。”
他们沉默地走了一阵子。
“你知道吗，我脑子里冒出了个可怕的念头，那小傻瓜八成爱上她了！”
“我早就有了个可怕的念头：*她*爱上*他*了。”
“那可就成了一桩糊涂透顶的事，”塔菲说。
他们继续往前走，反复琢磨着这两个可怕的念头，越是琢磨、思忖和回忆，他们就越确信两人的猜测都没错。
“这下可真乱成一锅粥了！”领主说，“——说到乱，咱们还是去吃午饭吧。”
他们沮丧得心烦意乱，以至于塔菲不知不觉吃了3份煎蛋卷，领主喝了2瓶半瓶装葡萄酒，塔菲喝了3瓶，那天下午他们整个下午都在外面游荡，生怕特里尔比会来画室——心里难过极了。
---
特里尔比之所以去卡雷尔的画室做模特，原委是这样的：
卡雷尔突然心血来潮，想在画室里待上一周（1 week），在学生中间画一幅人体，好让学生们观摩、并与他一同作画——如果可能的话，画得像他一样。于是他请特里尔比来做全裸人体模特，特里尔比欣然应允了。

===Original===

Then in five minutes or so Little Billee came bursting in, and as soon as he caught sight of her he stopped and stood as one petrified, his shoulders up, his eyes staring. Then lifting his arms, he turned and fled.
“*Qu’est ce qu’il a donc, ce Litrebili?*” exclaimed one or two students (for they had turned his English nickname into French).
“Perhaps he’s forgotten something,” said another. “Perhaps he’s forgotten to brush his teeth and part his hair!”
“Perhaps he’s forgotten to say his prayers!” said Barizel.
“He’ll come back, I hope!” exclaimed the master.
And the incident gave rise to no further comment.
But Trilby was much disquieted, and fell to wondering what on earth was the matter.
At first she wondered in French: French of the quartier latin. She had not seen Little Billee for a week, and wondered if he were ill. She had looked forward so much to his painting her﻿—painting her beautifully﻿—and hoped he would soon come back, and lose no time.
Then she began to wonder in English﻿—nice clean English of the studio in the Place St. Anatole des Arts﻿—her father’s English﻿—and suddenly a quick thought pierced her through and through, and made the flesh tingle on her insteps and the backs of her hands, and bathed her brow and temples with sweat.
She had good eyes, and Little Billee had a singularly expressive face.
Could it possibly be that he was *shocked* at seeing her sitting there?
She knew that he was peculiar in many ways. She remembered that neither he nor Taffy nor the Laird had ever asked her to sit for the figure, though she would have been only too delighted to do so for them. She also remembered how Little Billee had always been silent whenever she alluded to her posing for the “altogether,” as she called it, and had sometimes looked pained and always very grave.
She turned alternately pale and red, pale and red all over, again and again, as the thought grew up in her﻿—and soon the growing thought became a torment.
This newborn feeling of shame was unendurable﻿—its birth a travail that racked and rent every fibre of her moral being, and she suffered agonies beyond anything she had ever felt in her life.
“What is the matter with you, my child? Are you ill?” asked Carrel, who, like everyone else, was very fond of her, and to whom she had sat as a child (*l’Enfance de Psyché*, now in the Luxembourg Gallery, was painted from her).

===Chinese===

接着，过了大约5分钟光景，小比利风风火火地冲了进来，可一看到她，他便像石化了一般僵立在原地，双肩耸起，双眼圆睁。随后他扬起双臂，转身拔腿就逃。
“这个小比利到底怎么了？（*Qu’est ce qu’il a donc, ce Litrebili?*）”一两个学生惊呼道（他们把他原本的英文绰号改成了法语口吻）。
“也许他落了什么东西，”另一个学生说，“也许他忘了刷牙、分头发呢！”
“也许他忘了做祷告！”巴里泽尔（Barizel）嚷道。
“但愿他会回来！”大师喊道。
这场风波没有再引起更多的议论。
特里尔比却内心惶惶不安，止不住地思忖到底出了什么事。
起初她是用法语思索的：拉丁区的法语。她已经有一周没见到小比利了，不知他是不是病了。她一直满心期盼着他来给自己画像——把她画得美若天仙——并盼着他能早点回来，别耽搁工夫。
随后她开始用英语思忖——用圣阿纳托尔艺术广场画室里那种干净文雅的英语——她父亲的英语——突然间，一个念头闪电般刺穿了她，令她脚背和手背上的汗毛根根倒竖，额头和太阳穴渗满了冷汗。
她的眼力极佳，而小比利的脸上又向来写满了一切心思。
难道说，看到她赤身裸体坐在那里，他感到*震惊与排斥*？
她知道他在许多方面与众不同。她回想起无论是他、塔菲还是领主，从没要求过她充当人体模特，尽管她极乐意为他们宽衣解带。她还回想起，每当她提起自己当“全裸”（“altogether”）模特时，小比利总是默不作声，有时甚至流露出痛苦的神色，神情总是分外严肃。
随着这个念头在她心头蔓延，她浑身的脸色红一阵白一阵，反复交替——很快，这个疯长的念头就变成了无休止的折磨。
这种初生的羞耻感教人难以承受——它的诞生如同一场分娩阵痛，撕扯摧折着她道德灵魂的每一根神经，她经受着一生中从未体会过的剧烈痛苦。
“你怎么了，我的孩子？你病了吗？”卡雷尔关切地问道。他和所有人一样极喜欢她，她小时候就曾给他当过模特（如今陈列在卢森堡美术馆的《普绪喀的童年》[*l’Enfance de Psyché*]就是以她为模特画的）。

===Original===

She shook her head, and the work went on.
Presently she dropped her pitcher, that broke into bits; and putting her two hands to her face she burst into tears and sobs﻿—and there, to the amazement of everybody, she stood crying like a big baby﻿—*La source aux larmes?*
“What *is* the matter, my poor dear child?” said Carrel, jumping up and helping her off the throne.
“Oh, I don’t know﻿—I don’t know﻿—I’m ill﻿—very ill﻿—let me go home!”
And with kind solicitude and despatch they helped her on with her clothes, and Carrel sent for a cab and took her home.
And on the way she dropped her head on his shoulder, and wept, and told him all about it as well as she could, and Monsieur Carrel had tears in his eyes too, and wished to Heaven he had never induced her to sit for the figure, either then or at any other time. And pondering deeply and sorrowfully on such terrible responsibility (he had grown-up daughters of his own), he went back to the studio; and in an hour’s time they got another model and another pitcher, and went to work again.
---
And Trilby, as she lay disconsolate on her bed all that day and all the next, and all the next again, thought of her past life with agonies of shame and remorse that made the pain in her eyes seem as a light and welcome relief. For it came, and tortured worse and lasted longer than it had ever done before. But she soon found, to her miserable bewilderment, that mind-aches are the worst of all.
Then she decided that she must write to one of the trois Angliches, and chose the Laird.
She was more familiar with him than with the other two: it was impossible not to be familiar with the Laird if he liked one, as he was so easygoing and demonstrative, for all that he was such a canny Scot! Then she had nursed him through his illness; she had often hugged and kissed him before the whole studio full of people﻿—and even when alone with him it had always seemed quite natural for her to do so. It was like a child caressing a favorite young uncle or elder brother. And though the good Laird was the least susceptible of mortals, he would often find these innocent blandishments a somewhat trying ordeal! She had never taken such a liberty with Taffy; and as for Little Billee, she would sooner have died!
So she wrote to the Laird. I give her letter without the spelling, which was often faulty, although her nightly readings had much improved it:

===Chinese===

她摇了摇头，作画继续进行。
不一会儿，她手中的水罐滑落，摔得粉碎；她用双手捂住脸庞，放声痛哭抽泣起来——在全场所有人目瞪口呆的注视下，她站在那里像个大婴孩般嚎啕大哭——《泪泉》（*La source aux larmes?*）？
“到底是怎么了，我可怜的好孩子？”卡雷尔跳起身，扶她走下模特台。
“噢，我不知道——我不知道——我病了——病得很厉害——让我回家吧！”
大伙儿满怀温存与关切，手脚麻利地帮她穿上衣服，卡雷尔叫来一辆马车，亲自送她回家。
路上，她把头靠在卡雷尔肩头痛哭流涕，尽其所能把原委倾诉给他听；卡雷尔先生眼里也泛起了泪花，指天发誓但愿自己从未劝说过她充当人体模特，不论是此时还是以往任何时候。怀着对这份沉重罪责的深切悲痛与反思（他自己也有已成年的女儿），他回到了画室；不到1小时工夫，他们换了另一个模特和另一只水罐，重新开工作画。
---
特里尔比整天整夜、接连好几天心灰意冷地躺在床上，回首往事，心中的羞耻与悔恨令她觉得双眼的剧痛反而成了微不足道、令人宽慰的解脱。因为眼痛确实又发作了，比以往任何时候都要剧烈而持久。但她很快痛苦而困惑地发现，心头的剧痛才是世间最难忍受的折磨。
接着她打定主意，必须给三位英国佬中的某一位写封信，她挑选了领主。
她与领主最亲近随和：只要领主喜欢谁，谁就不可能不跟他无拘无束，尽管他是个精明的苏格兰人，为人却极其随和且真情流露！再者，领主生病时是她悉心照料的；她常常当着满画室众人的面拥抱他、亲吻他——哪怕单独和他相处时，她也觉得这样做再自然不过。这宛如孩童向喜爱的年轻叔叔或兄长撒娇。尽管好心肠的领主是个最不易动情的凡人，面对这些天真无邪的亲昵有时也觉得是一场颇为难熬的考验！对于塔菲，她从未如此放肆过；至于小比利，她宁死也不敢越雷池一步！
于是她给领主写了信。我省去了信中时常引人发笑的拼写错误，修剪掉冗余之词，以便于阅读：

===Original===

> “**My Dear Friend**﻿—I am very unhappy. I was sitting at Carrel’s, in the Rue des Potirons, and Little Billee came in, and was so shocked and disgusted that he ran away and never came back.
> “I saw it all in his face.
> “I sat there because M. Carrel asked me to. He has always been very kind to me﻿—M. Carrel﻿—ever since I was a child; and I would do anything to please him, but never *that* again.
> “He was there too.
> “I never thought anything about sitting before. I sat first as a child to M. Carrel. Mamma made me, and made me promise not to tell papa, and so I didn’t. It soon seemed as natural to sit for people as to run errands for them, or wash and mend their clothes. Papa wouldn’t have liked my doing that either, though we wanted the money badly. And so he never knew.
> “I have sat for the altogether to several other people besides﻿—M. Gérôme, Durien, the two Hennequins, and Émile Baratier; and for the head and hands to lots of people, and for the feet only to Charles Faure, André Besson, Mathieu Dumoulin, and Collinet. Nobody else.
> “It seemed as natural for me to sit as for a man. Now I see the awful difference.
> “And I have done dreadful things besides, as you must know﻿—as all the quartier knows. Baratier and Besson; but not Durien, though people think so. Nobody else, I swear﻿—except old Monsieur Penque at the beginning, who was mamma’s friend.
> “It makes me almost die of shame and misery to think of it; for that’s not like sitting. I knew how wrong it was all along﻿—and there’s no excuse for me, none. Though lots of people do as bad, and nobody in the quartier seems to think any the worse of them.
> “If you and Taffy and Little Billee cut me, I really think I shall go mad and die. Without your friendship I shouldn’t care to live a bit. Dear Sandy, I love your little finger better than any man or woman I ever met; and Taffy’s and Little Billee’s little fingers too.
> “What shall I do? I daren’t go out for fear of meeting one of you. Will you come and see me?
> “I am never going to sit again, not even for the face and hands. I am going back to be a *blanchisseuse de fin* with my old friend Angèle Boisse, who is getting on very well indeed, in the Rue des Cloîtres Ste. Pétronille.
> “You *will* come and see me, won’t you? I shall be in all day till you do.

===Chinese===

> “亲爱的桑迪，”信是这样写的——她愿意叫他桑迪就叫桑迪，愿意叫他领主就叫领主——“我一生中从没有这么痛苦过。我没脸来找你们，但我宁可用我10年、20年乃至30年的寿命，换来见你半个小时！
> “小比利为什么要那样拔腿就跑？是因为我吗？他是觉得被冒犯了吗？我真是个彻头彻尾的大蠢蛋！这两天我哭得双眼几乎都快瞎了。我知道我做错了，尽管是卡雷尔先生请我去做的。从我小时候起，卡雷尔先生就一直待我极好；为了让他高兴我什么都愿意做，但那种事我以后再也*不*干了。
> “当时他也在场。
> “我以前做模特从没多想过什么。我第一次做模特还是个小女孩，是给卡雷尔先生画。妈妈让我去的，还要我发誓不许告诉爸爸，所以我一直没说。很快，给别人当模特在我看来就跟替人跑腿、或者洗补衣裳一样平常自然。其实爸爸也不会赞成我去干那些事的，尽管当时我们极度缺钱。因此他从不知情。
> “除了他，我还给其他几个人当过全裸模特——热罗姆先生、迪里安、恩纳坎兄弟两人（the two Hennequins），还有埃米尔·巴拉蒂埃；给许多人单当过头部和手部的模特；只单给夏尔·福尔、安德烈·贝松、马蒂厄·迪穆兰和科利内当过脚模。除他们外再没别人了。
> “当时在我看来，做模特就像男人做模特一样天经地义。现在我才看清这其间可怕的鸿沟。
> “而且我还做过更可怕的荒唐事，你们想必也清楚——整个街区全都知道。我和巴拉蒂埃、贝松有过那种事；但和迪里安绝对没有，虽然外人都这么以为。我发誓没有别人了——除了一开始那位老先生庞克，他是我妈妈的朋友。
> “一想到这些，我几乎要羞愧悲痛得死去；因为那和做模特完全不是一回事。我一直都知道那是大错特错的——我没有任何借口，完全没有。尽管许多人干的坏事和我一样多，街区里似乎也没人因此看轻她们。
> “要是你、塔菲和小比利不理我了，我真会发疯死掉的。失去了你们的友谊，我一点儿也不想活了。亲爱的桑迪，我爱你的小手指头，胜过我所遇见的任何男人或女人；我也爱塔菲和小比利的小手指头。
> “我该怎么办呢？我不敢出门，生怕撞见你们中的任何一个。你愿意来看看我吗？
> “我再也不去当模特了，哪怕是当脸和手的模特也绝不去了。我要回去和我老朋友昂热勒·布瓦斯一起当精洗工（*blanchisseuse de fin*），她在圣佩特罗尼耶修道院街（Rue des Cloîtres Ste. Pétronille）的活计做得好极了。
> “你*一定会*来看我的，对不对？在你来之前，我会整天呆在家里等你。

===Original===

Or else I will meet you somewhere, if you will tell me where and when; or else I will go and see you in the studio, if you are sure to be alone. Please don’t keep me waiting long for an answer.
> “You don’t know what I’m suffering.
> “Your ever-loving, faithful friend,
> “Trilby O’Ferrall.”

===Chinese===

> 或者你要是告诉我时间和地点，我就去某个地方见你；或者如果你能确保只有你一个人在，我就去画室看你。请千万别让我苦等回信。
> “你不知道我心里有多苦。
> “永远爱你的忠实朋友，
> “特里尔比·奥费拉尔。”

===Original===

She sent this letter by hand, and the Laird came in less than ten minutes after she had sent it; and she hugged and kissed and cried over him so that he was almost ready to cry himself; but he burst out laughing instead﻿—which was better and more in his line, and very much more comforting﻿—and talked to her so nicely and kindly and naturally that by the time he left her humble attic in the Rue des Pousse-Cailloux her very aspect, which had quite shocked him when he first saw her, had almost become what it usually was.
The little room under the leads, with its sloping roof and mansard window, was as scrupulously neat and clean as if its tenant had been a holy sister who taught the noble daughters of France at some Convent of the Sacred Heart. There were nasturtiums and mignonette on the outer windowsill, and convolvulus was trained to climb round the window.
As she sat by his side on the narrow white bed, clasping and stroking his painty, turpentiny hand, and kissing it every five minutes, he talked to her like a father﻿—as he told Taffy afterwards﻿—and scolded her for having been so silly as not to send for him directly, or come to the studio. He said how glad he was, how glad they would all be, that she was going to give up sitting for the figure﻿—not, of course, that there was any real harm in it, but it was better not﻿—and especially how happy it would make them to feel she intended to live straight for the future. Little Billee was to remain at Barbizon for a little while; but she must promise to come and dine with Taffy and himself that very day, and cook the dinner; and when he went back to his picture, *Les Noces du Toréador*﻿—saying to her as he left, “*à ce soir donc, mille sacrés tonnerres de nong de Dew*!”﻿—he left the happiest woman in the whole Latin quarter behind him: she had confessed and been forgiven.
And with shame and repentance and confession and forgiveness had come a strange new feeling﻿—that of a dawning self-respect.
Hitherto, for Trilby, self-respect had meant little more than the mere cleanliness of her body, in which she had always revelled; alas! it was one of the conditions of her humble calling. It now meant another kind of cleanliness, and she would luxuriate in it forevermore; and the dreadful past﻿—never to be forgotten by her﻿—should be so lived down as in time, perhaps, to be forgotten by others.

===Chinese===

她派人亲手送去了这封信，信送出后不到10分钟，领主便赶了过来；她抱着他、亲着他，伏在他身上哭得伤心欲绝，领主自己也几乎要落下泪来；但他转而哈哈大笑起来——这更好，也更符合他的性格，带来的慰藉也深得多——他如此温存、亲切而自然地开导她，以至于当他离开普斯-卡尤街那间简陋的小阁楼时，起初见到她时那副令他心惊肉跳的憔悴神容，已然几乎恢复了往常的模样。
那间位于铅皮屋顶下、有着倾斜屋顶和孟莎式天窗的小房间，收拾得干干净净、井井有条，俨然就像圣心修道院里教导法兰西名门闺秀的修女闺房一般一尘不染。外侧窗台上栽着旱金莲和木犀草，牵牛花藤蔓顺着窗棂蜿蜒攀援。
她坐在窄小的白床边，握着、抚摸着领主那只沾满油彩与松节油气味的大手，每隔5分钟便亲吻一下，领主像一位慈父般开导着她——事后他正是这么对塔菲说的——责怪她太傻，为什么不立刻叫他过来，或者直接去画室找他们。他说得知她打算不再做人体模特，他有多高兴，大家会有多高兴——当然，并不是说做人体模特真有什么实质性的害处，但终归还是不当为好——尤其是想到她今后打算堂堂正正过日子，大家会感到无比欣慰。小比利还要在巴比松待上一小段时间；但她必须答应，今天晚上就过来跟塔菲和他一起吃晚饭，并且亲自下厨掌勺；当他回去继续画他的《斗牛士的婚礼》（*Les Noces du Toréador*）时——临别时对她嚷道：“今晚见啦，该死的老天爷啊！（*à ce soir donc, mille sacrés tonnerres de nong de Dew!*）”——他身后留下的，是整个拉丁区最幸福的女人：她忏悔了一切，并得到了宽恕。
伴随着羞愧、忏悔、坦白与宽恕，一种奇特的新情感在她心中油然而生——那就是初萌的自尊。
在这之前，对特里尔比而言，自尊充其量不过是指身体的洁净，她向来乐于保持身躯洁净；唉！这也是她那份卑微职业的必备条件之一。如今它代表着另一种洁净，她将永远在其中沉醉珍惜；而那可怕的过去——虽然她自己永世难忘——却可以通过堂堂正正的余生去化解，直到有朝一日，或许外人也会将其彻底遗忘。

===Original===

The dinner that evening was a memorable one for Trilby. After she had washed up the knives and forks and plates and dishes, and put them by, she sat and sewed. She wouldn’t even smoke her cigarette, it reminded her so of things and scenes she now hated. No more cigarettes for Trilby O’Ferrall.
They all talked of Little Billee. She heard about the way he had been brought up, about his mother and sister, the people he had always lived among. She also heard (and her heart alternately rose and sank as she listened) what his future was likely to be, and how rare his genius was, and how great﻿—if his friends were to be trusted. Fame and fortune would soon be his﻿—such fame and fortune as fell to the lot of very few﻿—unless anything should happen to spoil his promise and mar his prospects in life, and ruin a splendid career; and the rising of the heart was all for him, the sinking for herself. How could she ever hope to be even the friend of such a man? Might she ever hope to be his servant﻿—his faithful, humble servant?
---
Little Billee spent a month at Barbizon, and when he came back it was with such a brown face that his friends hardly knew him; and he brought with him such studies as made his friends “sit up.”
The crushing sense of their own hopeless inferiority was lost in wonder at his work, in love and enthusiasm for the workman.
Their Little Billee, so young and tender, so weak of body, so strong of purpose, so warm of heart, so light of hand, so keen and quick and piercing of brain and eye, was their master, to be stuck on a pedestal and looked up to and bowed down to, to be watched and warded and worshipped forevermore.
When Trilby came in from her work at six, and he shook hands with her and said “Hullo, Trilby!” her face turned pale to the lips, her underlip quivered, and she gazed down at him (for she was among the tallest of her sex) with such a moist, hungry, wide-eyed look of humble craving adoration that the Laird felt his worst fears were realized, and the look Little Billee sent up in return filled the manly bosom of Taffy with an equal apprehension.
Then they all four went and dined together at le père Trin’s, and Trilby went back to her *blanchisserie de fin*.

===Chinese===

那天的晚餐对特里尔比而言是终生难忘的。洗净收好刀叉、碗碟之后，她坐下来缝补衣物。她甚至连香烟都不肯抽了，那总让她想起自己如今深恶痛绝的旧景旧事。特里尔比·奥费拉尔自此彻底戒了烟。
他们谈论的都是小比利。她听说了他的成长历程、他的母亲和妹妹，以及他身边平日相处的人群。她还听说（在倾听时她的心头一阵阵忽起忽落）他未来可能拥有的光明前程，听闻他的天赋是多么举世罕见、多么卓绝超群——如果他的挚友们所言非虚的话。名利很快就会向他涌来——那是仅有极少数幸运儿才能享有的盛名与财富——除非有什么意外发生，断送了他的前程，毁掉他锦绣辉煌的生涯；心潮澎湃全是因为他，沉入谷底则全是因为她自己。她怎敢奢望有朝一日能成为这样一位人物的朋友呢？她是否能奢望充当他的仆人——一个忠心耿耿、谦卑卑微的仆人？
---
小比利在巴比松待了整整1个月，归来时晒得满脸黝黑，朋友们几乎都快认不出他了；他带回来的习作更是让朋友们“刮目相看”。
面对自己毫无指望的平庸所带来的沉重自卑感，在对他作品的由衷惊叹中、在对创作者本人的挚爱与热忱中烟消云散了。
他们的小比利，如此年少娇嫩，身体如此单薄，志向却如此坚定，胸怀如此炽热，笔触如此轻灵，头脑与目光如此敏锐迅捷、洞若观火，他就是他们的大师，理应被供奉在神坛上受人景仰膜拜，值得被他们永远守护、照料与崇拜。
傍晚6点特里尔比下工走进来时，小比利和她握手打了个招呼：“你好，特里尔比！”她的脸色白到了唇边，下唇微微颤抖，她俯下身注视着他（因为她在同性中身材算得上极高挑的），眼中含着泪水，流露出一种饥渴、圆睁双眸、卑微渴求的崇敬目光，领主见状顿时感到自己最害怕的担忧应验了；而小比利仰头回望的目光，亦让塔菲那魁梧的男子气胸膛充满了同等的忧虑。
随后他们4个人一起去特兰老爹（le père Trin）的小馆吃晚饭，饭后特里尔比便回到了自己的精洗作坊（*blanchisserie de fin*）。

===Original===

Next day Little Billee took his work to show Carrel, and Carrel invited him to come and finish his picture *The Pitcher Goes to the Well* at his own private studio﻿—an unheard-of favor, which the boy accepted with a thrill of proud gratitude and affectionate reverence.
So little was seen for some time of Little Billee at the studio in the Place St. Anatole des Arts, and little of Trilby; a *blanchisseuse de fin* has not many minutes to spare from her irons. But they often met at dinner. And on Sunday mornings Trilby came to repair the Laird’s linen and darn his socks and look after his little comforts, as usual, and spend a happy day. And on Sunday afternoons the studio would be as lively as ever, with the fencing and boxing, the piano-playing and fiddling﻿—all as it used to be.
And week by week the friends noticed a gradual and subtle change in Trilby. She was no longer slangy in French, unless it were now and then by a slip of the tongue, no longer so facetious and droll, and yet she seemed even happier than she had ever seemed before.
Also, she grew thinner, especially in the face, where the bones of her cheeks and jaw began to show themselves, and these bones were constructed on such right principles (as were those of her brow and chin and the bridge of her nose) that the improvement was astonishing, almost inexplicable.
Also, she lost her freckles as the summer waned and she herself went less into the open air. And she let her hair grow, and made of it a small knot at the back of her head, and showed her little flat ears, which were charming, and just in the right place, very far back and rather high; Little Billee could not have placed them better himself. Also, her mouth, always too large, took on a firmer and sweeter outline, and her big British teeth were so white and even that even Frenchmen forgave them their British bigness. And a new soft brightness came into her eyes that no one had ever seen there before. They were stars, just twin gray stars﻿—or rather planets just thrown off by some new sun, for the steady mellow light they gave out was not entirely their own.
Favorite types of beauty change with each succeeding generation.

===Chinese===

第二天，小比利带着自己的作品去给卡雷尔过目，卡雷尔邀请他去自己的私人画室完成那幅《汲水的水罐》（*The Pitcher Goes to the Well*）——这是一桩闻所未闻的殊荣，年轻的小比利带着满腔骄傲的感激与深切的崇敬接受了邀请。
因此有一阵子，在圣阿纳托尔艺术广场的画室里很少能见到小比利的踪影，也很少见到特里尔比；一名精洗女工从熨斗旁抽不出多少闲暇工夫。不过他们常在晚饭时相聚。而在周日上午，特里尔比一如既往地来替领主缝补亚麻衣裳、缝补袜子、照顾他的起居舒适，度过快乐的一天。到了周日下午，画室依然像以往一样热闹非凡，击剑、拳击、弹琴、拉小提琴——一切恢复如初。
每周复每周，朋友们逐渐察觉到特里尔比身上发生的微妙变化。她说法语时不再满嘴俚语，除非偶尔说溜了嘴；不再那么爱插科打诨和搞笑逗趣，可她看起来反而比以往任何时候都要快乐。
此外，她清瘦了许多，尤其是脸部，颧骨和下颌骨开始分明显露出来，而这些骨骼生得极为端正匀称（她的额头、下巴和鼻梁骨也是如此），以至于这种蜕变令人惊叹，近乎不可思议。
此外，随着夏意渐退，加上她自己较少出门暴晒，脸上的雀斑也消退了。她蓄长了头发，在脑后挽成一个小巧的发髻，露出了平整小巧的双耳，双耳娇俏可爱，位置恰到好处，生在偏后略高的正位上；哪怕小比利亲自构图，也定不出比这更完美的比例。此外，她那张向来嫌大的嘴唇勾勒出更坚定甜美的轮廓，她那一口硕大的英国牙齿洁白齐整，连法国人也原谅了牙齿的英式硕大。而她的眼眸中泛起了一种此前从未有人见过的柔和光彩。那是两颗星辰，恰似一对灰色的双子星——抑或是刚从某颗新恒星中脱胎而出的行星，因为它们散发出的恒常温润的光辉，并不完全属于它们自己。
世人钟爱的审美类型总在随一代又一代人而更迭。

===Original===

These were the days of Buckner’s aristocratic Album beauties, with lofty foreheads, oval faces, little aquiline noses, heart-shaped little mouths, soft dimpled chins, drooping shoulders, and long side ringlets that fell over them﻿—the Lady Arabellas and the Lady Clementinas, Musidoras and Medoras! A type that will perhaps come back to us some day.
May the present scribe be dead!
Trilby’s type would be infinitely more admired now than in the fifties. Her photograph would be in the shopwindows. Sir Edward Burne-Jones﻿—if I may make so bold as to say so﻿—would perhaps have marked her for his own, in spite of her almost too exuberant joyousness and irrepressible vitality. Rossetti might have evolved another new formula from her; Sir John Millais another old one of the kind that is always new and never sates nor palls﻿—like Clytie, let us say﻿—ever old and ever new as love itself!
Trilby’s type was in singular contrast to the type Gavarni had made so popular in the Latin quarter at the period we are writing of, so that those who fell so readily under her charm were rather apt to wonder why. Moreover, she was thought much too tall for her sex, and her day, and her station in life, and especially for the country she lived in. She hardly looked up to a bold gendarme! and a bold gendarme was nearly as tall as a *dragon de la garde*, who was nearly as tall as an average English policeman. Not that she was a giantess, by any means. She was about as tall as Miss Ellen Terry﻿—and that is a charming height, *I* think.
One day Taffy remarked to the Laird: “Hang it! I’m blest if Trilby isn’t the handsomest woman I know! She looks like a grande dame masquerading as a grisette﻿—almost like a joyful saint at times. She’s lovely! By Jove! I couldn’t stand her hugging me as she does you! There’d be a tragedy﻿—say the slaughter of Little Billee.”
“Ah! Taffy, my boy,” rejoined the Laird, “when those long sisterly arms are round my neck it isn’t *me* she’s hugging.”
“And then,” said Taffy, “what a trump she is! Why, she’s as upright and straight and honorable as a man! And what she says to one about one’s self is always so pleasant to hear! That’s Irish, I suppose. And, what’s more, it’s always true.”
“Ah, that’s Scotch!” said the Laird, and tried to wink at Little Billee, but Little Billee wasn’t there.
Even Svengali perceived the strange metamorphosis.

===Chinese===

那正是巴克纳画笔下贵族画册美人的鼎盛时代：饱满的高额头、鹅蛋脸、小巧的鹰钩鼻、心形小嘴、带着浅笑靥的下巴、削肩，以及披拂在肩头的垂卷发束——那些阿拉贝拉贵夫人、克莱门蒂娜贵小姐、穆西多拉与梅多拉！这种审美类型或许终有一天还会重新回潮。
但愿到那时在下已不在人世！
特里尔比这种身段样貌，在如今所受到的追捧赞叹，会远胜于50年代。她的玉照准会被陈列在橱窗里。爱德华·伯恩-琼斯爵士（Sir Edward Burne-Jones）——若我有幸妄加揣测的话——或许会视她为缪斯女神，尽管她身上散发着近乎过于洋溢的欢欣与无法遏制的活力。罗塞蒂（Rossetti）或许能从她身上提炼出全新的艺术定式；约翰·米莱斯爵士（Sir John Millais）则或许能挖掘出另一种历久弥新的传统韵味，永不让人厌倦餍足——不妨以克吕提厄（Clytie）为例——如爱情本身那般历久常新！
特里尔比的容貌体态，与我们在本书所描写时代里加瓦尔尼（Gavarni）在拉丁区风靡一时的典型画风形成了奇特的对比，以至于那些轻易为她倾倒的人往往还纳闷自己究竟为何着迷。不仅如此，在她那个时代、在她的出身阶层，尤其是在她生活的国度里，她的个头被认为对女人来说过于高挑。面对威风凛凛的宪兵，她几乎用不着抬头仰视！而一名威风的宪兵身材几乎与近卫龙骑兵（*dragon de la garde*）相当，后者又几乎与普通的英国巡警不相上下。当然，她绝非女巨人。她大约与埃伦·特里小姐（Miss Ellen Terry）一般高——而在我看来，这正是一个迷人的身量。
一天，塔菲对领主说：“见鬼！我敢说特里尔比是我认识的最漂亮的女人！她看起来宛如一位假扮成女工的贵妇人——有时简直像个满怀欢欣的圣徒。她美极了！老天爷！我可受不了她像拥抱你那样拥抱我！否则准得闹出悲剧来——比如血溅小比利。”
“啊！塔菲，我的好老弟，”领主回答道，“当那双姐姐般的长臂搂住我的脖子时，她抱的可不是*我*。”
“再说了，”塔菲说，“她是多么棒的人啊！瞧瞧，她为人正派、率真、光明磊落，丝毫不输男子汉！而且她当面夸人的话听起来总是那么熨帖受用！我想这大概是爱尔兰人的天性吧。更难得的是，那些话句句发自肺腑。”
“啊，那是苏格兰人的品格！”领主说，正想冲小比利眨眨眼，可小比利并不在场。
就连斯文加利也察觉到了这番奇特的蜕变。

===Original===

“*Ach*, Drilpy,” he would say, on a Sunday afternoon, “how beautiful you are! It drives me mad! I adore you. I like you thinner; you have such beautiful bones! Why do you not answer my letters? What! you do not *read* them? You *burn* them? And yet I﻿—*Donnerwetter!* I forgot! The grisettes of the quartier latin have not learned how to read or write; they have only learned how to dance the cancan with the dirty little pig-dog monkeys they call men. *Sacrement!* We will teach the little pig-dog monkeys to dance something else some day, we Germans. We will make music for them to dance to! Boum! boum! Better than the waiter at the Café de la Rotonde, *hein?* And the grisettes of the quartier latin shall pour us out your little white wine﻿—‘*fotre betit fin planc*,’ as your pig-dog monkey of a poet says, your rotten *verfluchter* De Musset, ‘who has got such a splendid future behind him’! Bah! What do *you* know of Monsieur Alfred de Musset? We have got a poet too, my Drilpy. His name is Heinrich Heine. If he’s still alive, he lives in Paris, in a little street off the Champs Élysées. He lies in bed all day long, and only sees out of one eye, like the Countess Hahn-Hahn, ha! ha! He adores French grisettes. He married one. Her name is Mathilde, and she has got *süssen füssen*, like you. He would adore you too, for your beautiful bones; he would like to count them one by one, for he is very playful, like me. And, *ach*! what a beautiful skeleton you will make! And very soon, too, because you do not smile on your madly-loving Svengali. You burn his letters without reading them! You shall have a nice little mahogany glass case all to yourself in the museum of the École de Médecine, and Svengali shall come in his new fur-lined coat, smoking his big cigar of the Havana, and push the dirty carabins out of the way, and look through the holes of your eyes into your stupid empty skull, and up the nostrils of your high bony sounding-board of a nose without either a tip or a lip to it, and into the roof of your big mouth, with your thirty-two big English teeth, and between your big ribs into your big chest, where the big leather lungs used to be, and say, ‘*Ach!* what a pity she had no more music in her than a big tomcat!’ And then he will look all down your bones to your poor crumbling feet, and say, ‘Ach! what a fool she was not to answer Svengali’s letters!’

===Chinese===

“哎，特里尔比（Drilpy），”某个周日下午他会这样说，“你现在多么美丽啊！真教我发狂！我崇拜你。我喜欢你更清瘦的模样；你的骨骼生得太美了！你为什么不回我的信？什么！你压根儿不*看*信？你把信全*烧*了？可我——该死（*Donnerwetter*）！我给忘了！拉丁区的女工们从来没学过读书写字；她们只学会了跟那帮被称作男人的肮脏猪狗猴子跳康康舞。真是见鬼（*Sacrement*）！终有一天，我们德国人会教这帮猪狗猴子跳点别的舞。我们会为他们演奏让他们跳舞的乐曲！咚！咚！比圆穹咖啡馆（Café de la Rotonde）的服务员弹得强多了，哈？拉丁区的女工们会为我们斟上你们清冽的白葡萄酒——‘你们那点劣质白葡萄酒（*fotre betit fin planc*）’，正如你们那位猪狗猴子诗人所写的那样，你们那位该下地狱（*verfluchter*）的缪塞，‘他的锦绣前程全都留在了过去身后’！呸！*你*又能懂什么阿尔弗雷德·德·缪塞先生？我们也有自己的诗人，我的特里尔比。他的名字叫海因里希·海涅。要是他现在还活着，就住在巴黎香榭丽舍大街旁的一条小街里。他整天躺在病床上，只有一只眼睛能看清东西，就像哈恩-哈恩伯爵夫人一样，哈哈！他极度迷恋法国女工。他还娶了一个。她叫玛蒂尔德，她也长着一对甜美迷人的双足（*süssen füssen*），就和你一样。他也会为你这副美丽的骨骼神魂颠倒的；他一定会喜欢一根一根数着你的骨头，因为他和我一样非常爱玩。哎！你将来会变成一具多么完美的骷髅骨架啊！而且很快就会变成那样，因为你不愿冲发疯般深爱着你的斯文加利微笑。你读都不读就烧掉了他的信！将来在医学院（École de Médecine）的博物馆里，你会独享一个精致的红木玻璃展柜，而斯文加利将穿着他的新皮草大衣，抽着哈瓦那大雪茄走进来，推开那些肮脏的医学生，透过你眼眶的空洞望进你那愚蠢空荡的颅骨，望进你那座没有鼻尖也没有嘴唇、高耸骨感的共鸣箱鼻孔，望进你那张长着32颗巨大的英国牙齿的大嘴上颚，从你粗大的肋骨间望进你空旷的胸膛——那里曾经长着一副厚皮肺叶——并叹道：‘哎！多可惜啊，她肚子里的音乐细胞还不如一只大公猫多！’然后他会顺着你的骨头一路打量到你可怜的

===Original===

and the dirty carabins shall﻿—”
“Shut up, you sacred fool, or I’ll precious soon spoil *your* skeleton for you.”
Thus the short-tempered Taffy, who had been listening.
Then Svengali, scowling, would play Chopin’s funeral march more divinely than ever; and where the pretty, soft part comes in, he would whisper to Trilby, “That is Svengali coming to look at you in your little mahogany glass case!”
And here let me say that these vicious imaginations of Svengali’s, which look so tame in English print, sounded much more ghastly in French, pronounced with a Hebrew-German accent, and uttered in his hoarse, rasping, nasal, throaty rook’s caw, his big yellow teeth baring themselves in a mongrel canine snarl, his heavy upper eyelids drooping over his insolent black eyes.
Besides which, as he played the lovely melody he would go through a ghoulish pantomime, as though he were taking stock of the different bones in her skeleton with greedy but discriminating approval. And when he came down to the feet, he was almost droll in the intensity of his terrible realism. But Trilby did not appreciate this exquisite fooling, and felt cold all over.
He seemed to her a dread, powerful demon, who, but for Taffy (who alone could hold him in check), oppressed and weighed on her like an incubus﻿—and she dreamed of him oftener than she dreamed of Taffy, the Laird, or even Little Billee!
---
Thus pleasantly and smoothly, and without much change or adventure, things went on till Christmastime.
Little Billee seldom spoke of Trilby, or Trilby of him. Work went on every morning at the studio in the Place St. Anatole des Arts, and pictures were begun and finished﻿—little pictures that didn’t take long to paint﻿—the Laird’s Spanish bullfighting scenes, in which the bull never appeared, and which he sent to his native Dundee and sold there; Taffy’s tragic little dramas of life in the slums of Paris﻿—starvings, drownings﻿—suicides by charcoal and poison﻿—which he sent everywhere, but did not sell.
Little Billee was painting all this time at Carrel’s studio﻿—his private one﻿—and seemed preoccupied and happy when they all met at mealtime, and less talkative even than usual.
He had always been the least talkative of the three; more prone to listen, and no doubt to think the more.

===Chinese===

那些肮脏的医学生将会——”
“闭嘴，你这不可救药的蠢货，否则我立马先砸烂*你的*骨架。”
一直在旁听着的急脾气塔菲厉声喝道。
于是斯文加利皱起眉头，神乎其神地弹起肖邦的《葬礼进行曲》；在旋律行进到优美柔和的段落时，他便悄声对特里尔比耳语道：“瞧，那是斯文加利走过来看望锁在红木玻璃柜里的你呢！”
在此请允许我说一句，斯文加利这些恶毒的幻想，在英文印成文字看似平淡无奇，但若用带着希伯来-德语腔调的法语吐露出来，配上他那嘶哑、刮擦、带有鼻音且喉音浓重的乌鸦般粗嘎嗓音，那一口发黄的大牙像杂种恶犬般龇牙咧嘴地呲露着，沉重的上眼皮耷拉在他傲慢无礼的黑眼珠上方，听起来要恐怖阴森得多。
不仅如此，当他弹奏那段优美的旋律时，还会做出食尸鬼般的哑剧动作，仿佛在带着贪婪却挑剔的赞许一一清点她骷髅上的每一块骨骼。而当他的目光落到脚骨上时，那可怕的逼真神态几乎显得滑稽可笑。特里尔比却丝毫领会不到这种精致恶作剧的妙处，只觉得通体冰凉。
在她看来，他宛如一个可怕而法力无边的恶魔；若非有塔菲在场（唯有塔菲能镇得住他），他就像梦魇般压得她喘不过气来——在梦里她梦见他的次数，甚至比梦见塔菲、领主抑或小比利还要频繁！
---
日子就这样平稳愉快、波澜不惊地流逝，直到圣诞节临近。
小比利很少谈起特里尔比，特里尔比也很少谈起小比利。圣阿纳托尔艺术广场画室每天早晨照常开工作画，一幅幅画作起草落笔并告完工——都是些用不了多少时间便能完成的小画——领主画的是西班牙斗牛场面，画中牛却从未露过面，这些画被他寄回老家邓迪并在那里变卖；塔菲画的则是巴黎贫民窟生活的悲剧小折子戏——饥馑、溺毙——烧炭与服毒自尽——他到处寄送，却一幅也没卖出去。
在此期间，小比利一直在卡雷尔的私人画室作画，每次大家碰面吃便饭时，他都显得若有所思又满心欢喜，言语比平时更为稀少。
在他们三人中，他向来是话最少的一个；更习惯于倾听，无疑也在作更深入的思考。

===Original===

In the afternoon people came and went as usual, and boxed and fenced and did gymnastic feats, and felt Taffy’s biceps, which by this time equalled Mr. Sandow’s!
Some of these people were very pleasant and remarkable, and have become famous since then in England, France, America﻿—or have died, or married, and come to grief or glory in other ways. It is the Ballad of the Bouillabaisse all over again!
It might be worth while my trying to sketch some of the more noteworthy, now that my story is slowing for a while﻿—like a French train when the engine-driver sees a long curved tunnel in front of him, as I do﻿—and no light at the other end!
My humble attempts at characterization might be useful as *mémoires pour servir* to future biographers. Besides, there are other reasons, as the reader will soon discover.
There was Durien, for instance﻿—Trilby’s especial French adorer, *pour le bon motif*! a son of the people, a splendid sculptor, a very fine character in every way﻿—so perfect, indeed, that there is less to say about him than any of the others﻿—modest, earnest, simple, frugal, chaste, and of untiring industry; living for his art, and perhaps also a little for Trilby, whom he would have been only too glad to marry. He was Pygmalion; she was his Galatea﻿—a Galatea whose marble heart would never beat for *him*!
Durien’s house is now the finest in the Parc Monceau; his wife and daughters are the best-dressed women in Paris, and he one of the happiest of men; but he will never quite forget poor Galatea:
“*La belle aux pieds d’albâtre﻿—aux deux talons de rose!*”
---
Then there was Vincent, a Yankee medical student, who could both work and play.
He is now one of the greatest oculists in the world, and Europeans cross the Atlantic to consult him. He can still play, and when he crosses the Atlantic himself for that purpose he has to travel incognito like a royalty, lest his play should be marred by work. And his daughters are so beautiful and accomplished that British dukes have sighed after them in vain. Indeed, these fair young ladies spend their autumn holiday in refusing the British aristocracy. We are told so in the society papers, and I can quite believe it. Love is not always blind; and if he is, Vincent is the man to cure him.

===Chinese===

下午，人们照常进进出出，练拳击、击剑、施展体操绝活，摩挲着塔菲的二头肌——此时他的臂力肌块已经不输山道先生（Mr. Sandow）了！
这些人当中，有些人极其讨人喜欢、出类拔萃，后来在英国、法国、美国声名鹊起——或者早已谢世、婚娶，以其他方式品尝了人间的悲苦与荣光。这完全就是《马赛鱼汤歌谣》（Ballad of the Bouillabaisse）的重演！
既然我的故事节奏暂时放缓——就像法国火车司机眼前出现了一条漫长弯曲的隧道一样，正如我眼前的景象——而隧道另一头还看不见光芒，或许值得我尝试描摹其中几位格外值得一提的人物！
我这番谦卑的人物速写，或许可以作为日后传记作家的参考备忘录（*mémoires pour servir*）。此外，还有其他缘由，读者很快就会明晓。
比如迪里安——特里尔比那位出于纯正动机（*pour le bon motif*）的法国仰慕者！他是人民的儿子，杰出的雕塑家，各方面品格皆极其出众——事实上完美得教人无从着墨——谦逊、热诚、朴素、节俭、贞洁且勤勉不怠；他为自己的艺术而生，或许也稍微为了特里尔比而活，他本会极其乐意迎娶她。他是皮格马利翁（Pygmalion）；她是他的伽拉忒亚（Galatea）——一位大理石般的心灵绝不会为*他*而跳动的伽拉忒亚！
如今迪里安在蒙梭公园（Parc Monceau）拥有最豪华的府邸；他的妻子和女儿们是巴黎衣着最考究的贵妇名媛，他本人也是世上最幸福的人之一；但他永远无法彻底忘却可怜的伽拉忒亚：
“拥有雪白石膏玉足、一对粉红脚跟的美人！（*La belle aux pieds d’albâtre—aux deux talons de rose!*）”
---
此外还有文森特（Vincent），一位既能埋头苦干又能尽情玩乐的美国医学生。
如今他已是享誉全球的顶级眼科名医，欧洲人纷纷横渡大西洋前去求医。他依然酷爱游玩，每当他自己横跨大西洋前来度假时，都不得不像王室贵族那般隐姓埋名，免得游兴被看诊打扰。他的女儿们生得如此明艳动人、才貌双全，以至于英国公爵们为之苦苦相思而不得。事实上，这些妙龄佳人秋季假期的主要消遣就是回绝英国贵族的求婚。社交界报刊如是说，对此我深信不疑。丘比特并不总是盲目的；退一步讲即便真是个盲人，文森特也正是能医好他的大夫。

===Original===

In those days he prescribed for us all round, and punched and stethoscoped us, and looked at our tongues for love, and told us what to eat, drink, and avoid, and even where to go for it.
For instance: late one night Little Billee woke up in a cold sweat, and thought himself a dying man﻿—he had felt seedy all day and taken no food; so he dressed and dragged himself to Vincent’s hotel, and woke him up, and said, “Oh, Vincent, Vincent! I’m a dying man!” and all but fainted on his bed. Vincent felt him all over with the greatest care, and asked him many questions. Then, looking at his watch, he delivered himself thus: “Humph! 3:30! rather late﻿—but still﻿—look here, Little Billee﻿—do you know the Halle, on the other side of the water, where they sell vegetables?”
“Oh yes! yes! What vegetable shall I﻿—”
“Listen! On the north side are two restaurants, Bordier and Baratte. They remain open all night. Now go straight off to one of those tuck shops, and tuck in as big a supper as you possibly can. Some people prefer Baratte. I prefer Bordier myself. Perhaps you’d better try Bordier first and Baratte after. At all events, lose no time; so off you go!”
Thus he saved Little Billee from an early grave.
---
Then there was the Greek, a boy of only sixteen, but six feet high, and looking ten years older than he was, and able to smoke even stronger tobacco than Taffy himself, and color pipes divinely; he was a great favorite in the Place St. Anatole, for his bonhomie, his niceness, his warm geniality. He was the capitalist of this select circle (and nobly lavish of his capital). He went by the name of Poluphloisboiospaleapologos Petrilopetrolicoconose﻿—for so he was christened by the Laird﻿—because his real name was thought much too long and much too lovely for the quartier latin, and reminded one of the Isles of Greece﻿—where burning Sappho loved and sang.
What was he learning in the Latin quarter? French? He spoke French like a native! Nobody knows. But when his Paris friends transferred their bohemia to London, where were they ever made happier and more at home than in his lordly parental abode﻿—or fed with nicer things?
That abode is now his, and lordlier than ever, as becomes the dwelling of a millionaire and city magnate; and its gray-bearded owner is as genial, as jolly, and as hospitable as in the old Paris days, but he no longer colors pipes.

===Chinese===

在当年，他常免费为我们大家问诊开方，又是敲打叩诊，又是用听诊器听胸腔，出于友情查看我们的舌苔，叮嘱我们该吃什么、喝什么、忌什么，甚至告诉我们该去哪里弄这些吃喝。
比如：“熬夜苦读、作画或在屋里通宵狂欢，通常是极大的浪费时间，因为肺部在夜间需要臭氧；只有在午夜12点以后，大伙儿全睡下了，臭氧才会冒出来。可如果你非得熬夜不可，你在忙些什么呢？”
“给老朋友写一首歌。”
“那好办；多喝水，千万别喝茶或咖啡，吃饼干，别吃糖；因为糖会导致胆汁发酵，胆汁上逆到眼里就会教你心情烦躁，那时你就非得睡觉不可了；而饼干会让你口渴，你就会喝水，水能涤荡你的肾脏，让你神清气爽，这样你就能继续熬下去了。”
文森特真是个不可多得的好伙伴，带有一股冷面幽默的美国做派！
---
还有一位名字长得拗口的希腊人，他用白垩粉笔在灰纸上临摹古代雕像，一个下午就能画出40或50幅，在他画室的墙壁四周钉着30或40幅这样的速写；这些画没有画商肯出钱买，却无不展露出过人的天赋，哪怕谈不上多么勤奋。他是一位货真价实的古希腊传人，有着古典的侧颜轮廓、乌黑卷曲的头发、一双硕大的黑眼睛，以及一撮精致的金色小胡须。他的名字叫彼得，但大家管他叫彼得罗利科科诺斯（Petrolicoconose）。
他穷得叮当响，经常被房东撵出住处。他靠干面包和清水度日，全凭记忆在于谢特街（Rue de la Huchette）阁楼清冷的光线中描绘帕特农神庙的景致；那些画虽无一人问津，有时却颇具迷人魅力。他只有一个坏毛病：为了挣钱，他会替人给海泡石烟斗熏色，一连30或40个小时不停歇，然后把挣来的钱全给街上的孩子们买糖果吃。他自己本身就是一个无害、风趣又古怪的孩童！可怜的家伙，如今他早已不在人世，再也不能替烟斗熏色了。

===Original===

---
Then there was Carnegie, fresh from Balliol, redolent of the ’varsity. He intended himself then for the diplomatic service, and came to Paris to learn French as it is spoke; and spent most of his time with his fashionable English friends on the right side of the river, and the rest with Taffy, the Laird, and Little Billee on the left. Perhaps that is why he has not become an ambassador. He is now only a rural dean, and speaks the worst French I know, and speaks it wherever and whenever he can.
It serves him right, I think.
He was fond of lords, and knew some (at least, he gave one that impression), and often talked of them, and dressed so beautifully that even Little Billee was abashed in his presence. Only Taffy, in his threadbare out-at-elbow shooting-jacket and cricket cap, and the Laird, in his tattered straw hat and Taffy’s old overcoat down to his heels, dared to walk arm in arm with him﻿—nay, insisted on doing so﻿—as they listened to the band in the Luxembourg Gardens.
And his whiskers were even longer and thicker and more golden than Taffy’s own. But the mere sight of a boxing-glove made him sick.
---
Then there was the yellow-haired Antony, a Swiss﻿—the idle apprentice, *le “roi des truands*,” as we called him﻿—to whom everything was forgiven, as to François Villon, *à cause de ses gentillesses* surely, for all his reprehensible pranks, the gentlest and most lovable creature that ever lived in bohemia, or out of it.
Always in debt, like Svengali﻿—for he had no more notion of the value of money than a hummingbird, and gave away in reckless generosity to friends what in strictness belonged to his endless creditors﻿—like Svengali, humorous, witty, and a most exquisite and original artist, and also somewhat eccentric in his attire (though scrupulously clean), so that people would stare at him as he walked along﻿—a thing that always gave him dire offence! But unlike Svengali, full of delicacy, refinement, and distinction of mind and manner﻿—void of any self-conceit﻿—and, in spite of the irregularities of his life, the very soul of truth and honor, as gentle as he was chivalrous and brave﻿—the warmest, stanchest, sincerest, most unselfish friend in the world; and, as long as his purse was full, the best and drollest boon companion in the world﻿—but that was not forever!

===Chinese===

---
此外还有卡耐基（Carnegie），刚从牛津贝利奥尔学院毕业，满身散发着名牌大学的书卷气。他当时立志投身外交界，便来到巴黎学习地道的法语口语；他大部分时间都与塞纳河右岸的时髦英国朋友混在一起，其余时间则在左岸与塔菲、领主和小比利为伍。也许这正是他未能当上大使的缘由。如今他不过是个乡间教区长，操着一口我所听过的最烂的法语，而且随时随地都要讲上几句。
依我看，这是他罪有应得。
他热衷攀附贵族老爷，也确实结识了几位（至少他给人这种印象），嘴里常把他们挂在心上，平日穿戴得如此光鲜亮丽，连小比利站在他面前都自惭形秽。唯有塔菲穿着那件肘部磨破的旧猎装和板球帽，领主顶着破草帽、拖着塔菲那件垂到脚跟的旧大衣，敢于同他手挽手并肩漫步——甚至坚持这么做——一边在卢森堡公园里聆听军乐队演奏。
他的连鬓胡须比特里尔比或塔菲本人的还要更长、更密、更金黄。然而一看到拳击手套，他便恶心得想吐。
---
接着是黄头发的瑞士人安东尼（Antony）——那个游手好闲的学徒，我们称他为“乞丐帮之王”（*le “roi des truands”*）——人们对他的一切过失都加以宽容，就像当年宽容弗朗索瓦·维庸一样，想必是看在他为人可爱的份上（*à cause de ses gentillesses*）；尽管他那些恶作剧为人诟病，但他绝对是波希米亚圈内圈外有史以来最温柔、最可爱的人儿。
他和斯文加利一样债台高筑——因为他对金钱价值的概念还不如一只蜂鸟多，常常慷慨得不计后果，把严格属于无尽债主的钱财随手散给朋友——也和斯文加利一样幽默风趣，是一位极其精湛独创的艺术家，着装也略显古怪（尽管一尘不染），以至于路人走过时常驻足注视他——这总教他深感冒犯！但与斯文加利截然不同的是，他举手投足与谈吐心思充满细腻、优雅与尊贵——没有半点狂妄自大——尽管生活放荡不羁，骨子里却极为诚实崇高，既温柔敦厚又兼具骑士风度和勇敢气魄——他是世上最热情、最忠诚、最真挚、最无私的朋友；只要囊中有钱，他也是世上最好、最风趣的酒肉密友——可惜他的钱袋并不总能装满！

===Original===

When the money was gone, then would Antony hie him to some beggarly attic in some lost Parisian slum, and write his own epitaph in lovely French or German verse﻿—or even English (for he was an astounding linguist); and, telling himself that he was forsaken by family, friends, and mistress alike, look out of his casement over the Paris chimney-pots for the last time, and listen once more to “the harmonies of nature,” as he called it﻿—and “aspire towards the infinite,” and bewail “the cruel deceptions of his life”﻿—and finally lay himself down to die of sheer starvation.
And as he lay and waited for his release that was so long in coming, he would beguile the weary hours by mumbling a crust “watered with his own salt tears,” and decorating his epitaph with fanciful designs of the most exquisite humor, pathos, and beauty﻿—these illustrated epitaphs of the young Antony, of which there exists a goodly number, are now priceless, as all collectors know all over the world.
Fainter and fainter would he grow﻿—and finally, on the third day or thereabouts, a remittance would reach him from some long-suffering sister or aunt in far Lausanne﻿—or else the fickle mistress or faithless friend (who had been looking for him all over Paris) would discover his hiding-place, the beautiful epitaph would be walked off in triumph to le père Marcas in the Rue du Ghette and sold for twenty, fifty, a hundred francs﻿—and then Vogue la galére! And back again to bohemia, dear bohemia and all its joys, as long as the money lasted﻿ ﻿… *e poi, da capo!*
And now that his name is a household word in two hemispheres, and he himself an honor and a glory to the land he has adopted as his own, he loves to remember all this and look back from the lofty pinnacle on which he sits perched up aloft to the impecunious days of his idle apprenticeship﻿—*le bon temps où l’on ètait si malheureux!*
And with all that quixotic dignity of his, so famous is he as a wit that when he jokes (and he is always joking) people laugh first, and then ask what he was joking about. And you can even make your own mild funniments raise a roar by merely prefacing them “as Antony once said!”
The present scribe has often done so.
And if by a happy fluke you should some day hit upon a really good thing of your own﻿—good enough to be quoted﻿—be sure it will come back to you after many days prefaced “as Antony once said.”

===Chinese===

一旦银钱告罄，安东尼便会躲进巴黎某个无人知晓的贫民窟简陋阁楼里，用优美的法语或德语诗句写下自己的墓志铭——甚至是用英语（因为他是个令人惊叹的语言天才）；他告诫自己已被家人、朋友和情妇统统抛弃，最后一次透过窗户眺望巴黎的烟囱管，再倾听一次被他称作“大自然的和谐交响”——并“向着无垠的苍穹心驰神往”，哀叹自己“一生所遭遇的残酷欺骗”——最后躺平下来，准备活活饿死。
当他躺在那里等待那迟迟不来的解脱时，便一边啃着“浸透咸涩泪水”的面包干消磨漫长难耐的时光，一边在自己的墓志铭四周装饰以构思极其奇妙、兼具幽默、凄美与雅致的精美插画——青年安东尼留下的这批带插画的墓志铭数量颇丰，如今全天下的收藏家都知道它们已然价值连城。
他会变得越来越虚弱——最终，大约到了第3天光景，一笔汇款总会从远在洛桑（Lausanne）、饱受折磨的姐妹或姑母那里寄达——要不就是那个寻遍全巴黎的薄情情妇或负心朋友终于找到了他的藏身之所，那张美丽的墓志铭便会被得意洋洋地送到盖特街（Rue du Ghette）的马尔卡老爹（le père Marcas）那里，卖上20、50乃至100法郎——接着，便尽情快活吧（*Vogue la galère!*）！只要手头还有银子，就重返波希米亚，重返可爱的波希米亚和它无尽的欢愉……随后周而复始（*e poi, da capo!*）！
如今，他的名字在东西两个半球家喻户晓，他本人也成了他所归属之地的荣耀与骄傲，他极爱回想这一切，从自己高高屹立的荣耀顶峰，回顾昔日游手好闲学徒生涯中那些囊空如洗的日子——“当年我们那般凄惨，岁月却这般美好！（*le bon temps où l’on était si malheureux!*）”
伴随着他那种唐·吉诃德式的尊严，他作为大机智家的名声是如此响亮，以至于每逢他开玩笑（他无时无刻不在开玩笑），人们总是先哄堂大笑，然后再去打听他到底讲了些什么笑料。甚至你自己说句不痛不痒的俏皮话，只要在开头冠上一句“正如安东尼曾说的！”便能引得满堂喝彩。
在下本人就屡试不爽。
假若某天你撞了大运，自己灵机一动想出了一句真正精彩的妙语——精彩得足以教人传诵——大可放心，过不了多久，这句话准会披着“正如安东尼曾说的”的前缀，重新传回你的耳朵里。

===Original===

And these jokes are so good-natured that you almost resent their being made at anybody’s expense but your own﻿—never from Antony

> “The aimless jest that striking has caused pain,

The idle word that he’d wish back again!”

===Chinese===

这些玩笑是如此厚道和善，以至于除了拿你自己开涮之外，无论他拿谁开玩笑你都会觉得心有不忍——安东尼嘴里绝不会吐出：

> “漫无边际的玩笑刺痛了旁人的心怀，

徒劳的闲言碎语教人巴不得收回不再来！”

===Original===

Indeed, in spite of his success, I don’t suppose he ever made an enemy in his life.
And here, let me add (lest there be any doubt as to his identity), that he is now tall and stout and strikingly handsome, though rather bald﻿—and such an aristocrat in bearing, aspect, and manner that you would take him for a blue-blooded descendant of the crusaders instead of the son of a respectable burgher in Lausanne.
---
Then there was Lorrimer, the industrious apprentice, who is now also well-pinnacled on high; himself a pillar of the Royal Academy﻿—probably, if he lives long enough, its future president﻿—the duly knighted or baroneted Lord Mayor of “all the plastic arts” (except one or two perhaps, here and there, that are not altogether without some importance).
